**Arabs** ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): عَرَب)[^cite_note-59] are an [ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_group)[^cite_note-78] mainly inhabiting the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) and [North Africa](21714.md). A significant [Arab diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_diaspora) is present in various parts of the world.[^cite_note-79] Before the spread of [Arabic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) in the wake of the [Arab conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_conquests), "Arab" largely referred to the [Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_peoples) inhabitants—both settled and nomadic—of the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) and the [Syrian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Desert).[^cite_note-Britannica-80] In modern usage, it includes people from across the [Greater Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_Middle_East) that share Arabic as a [native language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_language).[^cite_note-Britannica-80]

Arabs have been in the [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent) for thousands of years.[^cite_note-81] In the 9th century BCE, the [Assyrians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria) made written references to Arabs as inhabitants of the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant), [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), and [Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia).[^cite_note-82] Throughout the [Ancient Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Near_East), Arabs established influential civilizations starting from 3000 BCE onwards, such as [Dilmun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilmun), [Gerrha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrha), and [Magan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magan_\(civilization\)), playing a vital role in trade between Mesopotamia, and the [Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Mediterranean_region).[^cite_note-83] Other prominent [tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia) include [Midian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midian), [ʿĀd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BF%C4%80d), and [Thamud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thamud) mentioned in the [Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) and [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran). Later, in 900 BCE, the [Qedarites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qedarites) enjoyed close relations with the nearby [Canaanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan#Canaanites) and [Aramaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaeans) states, and their territory extended from [Lower Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Egypt) to the [Southern Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Levant).[^cite_note-84] From 1200 BCE to 110 BCE, powerful kingdoms emerged such as [Saba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabaeans), [Lihyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lihyan), [Ma'in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'in), [Qataban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qataban), [Hadhramaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hadhramaut), [Awsan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Awsan), and [Himyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyar).[^cite_note-85] According to the [Abrahamic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abrahamic_religions) tradition, Arabs are descendants of [Abraham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham) through his son [Ishmael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishmael).[^cite_note-86]

During [classical antiquity](252905.md), the [Nabataeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataeans) established their [kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_Kingdom) with [Petra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petra) as the capital in 300 BCE,[^cite_note-87] by 271 CE, the [Palmyrene Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Empire) with the capital [Palmyra](46709309.md), led by Queen [Zenobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenobia), encompassed [Syria Palaestina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_Palaestina), [Arabia Petraea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Petraea), [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt_\(Roman_province\)), and large parts of [Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolia).[^cite_note-waro-88] The Arab or [Aramean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arameans) [Itureans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iturea) inhabited [Lebanon](17771.md), [Syria](7515849.md), and northern Palestine ([Galilee](12639.md)) during the [Hellenistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hellenistic_period) and Roman periods.[^cite_note-Berndt_Schaller_14922-89] The [Osroene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osroene) and [Hatran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hatra) were Arab-ruled kingdoms in [Upper Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Mesopotamia) around 200 CE.[^cite_note-Hatra_and_the_Parthian_Commonwealth2-90] In 164 CE, the [Sasanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) called part of upper Mesopotamia "*[Arbayistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbayistan)*", meaning "land of the Arabs,"[^cite_note-oxfordreference.com2-91] having conquered the land from the previously Jewish [Adiabene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiabene).[^cite_note-oxfordreference.com2-91] The probably Arab [Emesenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emesene_dynasty) ruled [Emesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emesa) ([Homs](77533.md)), [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Syria) by 46 BCE.[^cite_note-92] During [late antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_antiquity), the [Tanukhids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanukhids), [Salihids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salihids), [Lakhmids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmid_kingdom), [Kinda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kinda), and [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids) were dominant Arab tribes in the Levant, Mesopotamia, and Arabia; they predominantly embraced [Christianity](5211.md).[^cite_note-93]

During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam) fostered a vast Arab union, leading to significant [Arab migrations to the Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migrations_to_the_Maghreb), [the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migrations_to_the_Levant), and neighbouring territories under the rule of Arab empires such as the [Rashidun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun), [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate), [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate), and [Fatimid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate), ultimately leading to the decline of the [Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) and [Sasanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia) empires. At its peak, [Arab territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) stretched from [southern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_France) [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_France) to western [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_China), forming one of [history's largest empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_empires).[^cite_note-94] The [Great Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt) in the early 20th century aided in [dismantling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire) the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md), ultimately leading to the formation of the [Arab League](52625.md) on 22 March 1945, with its [Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_of_the_Arab_League) endorsing the principle of a "[unified Arab homeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism)".[^cite_note-95]

Arabs from [Morocco](19291.md) to [Iraq](7515928.md) share a common bond based on ethnicity, [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic), [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_culture), [history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Arabs), [identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_identity), [ancestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia), [nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism), [geography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), [unity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism), and [politics](14496822.md),[^cite_note-96] which give the region a distinct identity and distinguish it from other parts of the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world).[^cite_note-97] They also have their own customs, [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_literature), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_music), [dance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_folk_dances), [media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_culture#Media), [food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_cuisine), [clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_clothing), society, [sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_policies_of_the_Arab_League), [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_architecture), [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_art) and, [mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia).[^cite_note-98] Arabs have significantly influenced and contributed to [human progress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progress) in many fields, including [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_science), [technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_inventions_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_philosophy), [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_ethics), literature, politics, [business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_economy), [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_Art), music, [comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_humor), theatre, [cinema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_cinema), architecture, food, [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_medicine), and [religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Islam#Origins_of_Islam).[^cite_note-99] [Before Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia), most Arabs followed [polytheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytheistic) [Semitic religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Semitic_religion), while some [tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_tribes_of_Arabia) adopted [Judaism](15624.md) or [Christianity](5211.md) and a few individuals, known as the *[hanifs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanif)*, followed a form of [monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism).[^cite_note-auto-100] Currently, around [93% of Arabs are Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Muslims), while the [rest are mainly Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians), as well as Arab groups of [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) and [Baháʼís](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%ADs).[^cite_note-PharesIntro-101]

## Etymology

Further information: [Etymology of Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etymology_of_Arab)



The [Namara inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namara_inscription) is an Arabic epitaph in [Nabataean script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_script) of [Imru' al-Qais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imru'_al-Qais), son of "Amr, king of all the Arabs". Basalt, found at [Nimreh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nimreh) in the [Hauran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauran) ([Southern Syria](498693.md)), dated 7 December 328 CE.

The earliest documented use of the word *Arab* in reference to a people appears in the [Kurkh Monoliths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurkh_Monoliths), an [Akkadian-language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_language) record of the [Assyrian conquest of Aram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyrian_conquest_of_Aram) (9th century BCE). The Monoliths used the term to refer to [Bedouins](61770.md) of the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) under [King Gindibu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gindibu), who fought as part of a coalition opposed to [Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria).[^cite_note-Retsop105-102]

The related word *ʾaʿrāb* is used to refer to Bedouins today, in contrast to *ʿArab* which refers to Arabs in general.[^cite_note-103] Both terms are mentioned around 40 times in pre-Islamic [Sabaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabaean_language) inscriptions. The term *ʿarab* ('Arab') occurs also in the titles of the [Himyarite kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite_Kingdom) from the time of ['Abu Karab Asad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tubba_Abu_Karab_As'ad) until MadiKarib Ya'fur. According to Sabaean grammar, the term *ʾaʿrāb* is derived from the term *ʿarab*. The term is also mentioned in [Quranic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) verses, referring to people who were living in [Madina](36636.md) and it might be a south Arabian [loanword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanword) into Quranic language.[^cite_note-104]

The oldest surviving indication of an Arab national identity is an inscription made in an archaic form of Arabic in 328 CE using the [Nabataean alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_alphabet), which refers to [Imru' al-Qays ibn 'Amr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imru'_al-Qays_ibn_'Amr) as 'King of all the Arabs'.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106] [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus) refers to the Arabs in the Sinai, southern Palestine, and the [frankincense region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_of_Frankincense) (Southern Arabia). Other Ancient-Greek historians like [Agatharchides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agatharchides), [Diodorus Siculus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diodorus_Siculus) and [Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo) mention Arabs living in [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) (along the [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates)), in Egypt (the Sinai and the Red Sea), southern Jordan (the [Nabataeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataeans)), the [Syrian steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_steppe) and in eastern Arabia (the people of [Gerrha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrha)). Inscriptions dating to the 6th century BCE in Yemen include the term 'Arab'.[^cite_note-107]

The most popular Arab account holds that the word *Arab* came from an [eponymous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eponym) father named [Ya'rub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ya'rub), who was supposedly the first to speak Arabic. [Abu Muhammad al-Hasan al-Hamdani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Muhammad_al-Hasan_al-Hamdani) had another view; he states that Arabs were called *gharab* ('westerners') by [Mesopotamians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia) because Bedouins originally resided to the west of Mesopotamia; the term was then corrupted into *Arab*.

Yet another view is held by [al-Masudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Masudi) that the word *Arab* was initially applied to the [Ishmaelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishmaelites) of the [Arabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabah) valley. In the [Hebrew Bible's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) etymology, *Arab* ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): ערבי, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Hebrew): *arvi*) comes from the desert origin of the Bedouins whom it originally described as *arava* ([Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language): ערבה), meaning 'desert' or 'wilderness.'[^cite_note-108]

The root *ʿ-r-b* has several additional meanings in Semitic languages—including 'west, sunset', 'desert', 'mingle', 'mixed', 'merchant' and 'raven'—and are "comprehensible" with all of these having varying degrees of relevance to the emergence of the name. It is also possible that some forms were [metathetical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metathesis_\(linguistics\)) from *ʿ-B-R*, 'moving around' (Arabic: *ʿ-B-R*, 'traverse') and hence, it is alleged, 'nomadic'.[^cite_note-109]

## Origins

Further information: [Pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia), [Proto-Arabic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Arabic_language), and [Old Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Arabic)

See also: [Generations of Noah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generations_of_Noah), [Ishmaelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishmaelites), and [Qahtanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qahtanite)

Arabic is a Semitic language that belongs to the [Afroasiatic language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afroasiatic_languages). The majority of scholars accept the "[Arabian Peninsula](47858.md)" has long been accepted as the original *[Urheimat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urheimat)* (linguistic homeland) of the [Semitic languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_languages).[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-111][^cite_note-112][^cite_note-113] with some scholars investigating if its origins are in the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant).[^cite_note-114] The [ancient Semitic-speaking peoples](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Semitic-speaking_peoples) lived in the [ancient Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Near_East), including the Levant, [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), and the Arabian Peninsula from the 3rd millennium BCE to the end of antiquity. [Proto-Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Semitic_language) likely reached the Arabian Peninsula by the 4th millennium BCE, and its daughter languages spread outward from there,[^cite_note-\:0-115] while [Old Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Arabic) began to differentiate from Central Semitic by the start of the 1st millennium BCE.[^cite_note-academia.edu-116] [Central Semitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Semitic_languages) is a branch of the Semitic language includes Arabic, [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic), the [Canaanite languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaanite_languages) ([Ammonite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonite_language), [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language), [Moabite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moabite_language), [Philistine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philistine_language), [Phoenician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenician_language), etc.) and others.[^cite_note-117][^cite_note-academia.edu3-118] The origins of Proto-Semitic may lie in the Arabian Peninsula, with the language spreading from there to other regions. This theory proposes that Semitic peoples reached Mesopotamia and other areas from the deserts to the west, such as the [Akkadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akkadian_Empire) who entered Mesopotamia around the late 4th millennium BCE.[^cite_note-\:0-115] The origins of Semitic peoples are thought to include various regions Mesopotamia, the Levant, the Arabian Peninsula, and [North Africa](21714.md). Some view that Semitic may have originated in the Levant around 3800 BCE and subsequently spread to the Horn of Africa around 800 BCE from Arabia, as well as to North Africa.[^cite_note-AK1-119][^cite_note-120]

According to Arab–[Islamic–Jewish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic%E2%80%93Jewish_relations) traditions, [Ishmael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishmael), the son of Abraham and [Hagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagar) was "father of the Arabs".[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-EoR-Ishmael-122][^cite_note-autogenerated1-123][^cite_note-Sajjadi_20152-124][^cite_note-bbc2-125] Ishmael was considered the ancestor of the Islamic prophet [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad), the founder of [Islam](6037917.md). The tribes of Central West Arabia called themselves the "people of Abraham and the offspring of Ishmael."[^cite_note-126] [Ibn Khaldun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Khaldun), an Arab scholar in the 8th century, described the Arabs as having Ishmaelite origins.[^cite_note-Levity-127]

The [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) mentions that [Ibrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_in_Islam) (Abraham) and his wife [Hajar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagar_in_Islam) (Hagar) bore a [prophetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophecy) child named Ishmael, who was gifted by [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allah) a favor above other people.[^cite_note-128] Ibrahim and Ishmael built the [Kaaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaba) in Mecca, which was originally constructed by [Adam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam).[^cite_note-129] According to the [Samaritan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritan) book [Asaṭīr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Asatir):[^cite_note-Gaster-130]<sup>: 262 </sup> "And after the death of Abraham, Ishmael reigned twenty-seven years; And all the children of [Nebaot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nebaioth) ruled for one year in the lifetime of Ishmael; And for thirty years after his death from the [river of Egypt](21244.md) to the river [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates); and they built [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca)."[^cite_note-131] The [Targum Onkelos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Targum_Onkelos) annotates ([Genesis 25\:16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledot)), describing the extent of their settlements: The Ishmaelites lived from Hindekaia (India) to Chalutsa (possibly in Arabia), by the side of [Mizraim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mizraim) (Egypt), and from the area around Arthur ([Assyria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria)) up towards the north. This description suggests that the Ishmaelites were a widely dispersed group with a presence across a significant portion of the ancient Near East.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133]

## History

Main article: [History of the Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Arabs)





Relief from Aššur-bāni-apli's palace depicting Assyrian soldiers pursuing camel-riding Qedarite Arab warriors.

The nomads of Arabia had been spreading through the desert fringes of the [Fertile Crescent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertile_Crescent) since at least 3000 BCE, but the first known reference to the Arabs as a distinct group is from an Assyrian scribe recording the [Battle of Qarqar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Qarqar) in 853 BCE.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] The history of the Arabs during the pre-Islamic period covers various regions such as [Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia), Levant, Mesopotamia, and Egypt. The Arabs were mentioned by their neighbors, such as [Assyrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assyria) and [Babylonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylonia) Royal Inscriptions from 9th to 6th century BCE.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139] There are also records from [Sargon's reign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sargon_of_Akkad) that mention sellers of iron to people called Arabs in Ḫuzaza in [Babylon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Babylon), causing Sargon to prohibit such trade out of fear that the Arabs might use the resource to manufacture weapons against the Assyrian army. The history of the Arabs in relation to the Bible shows that they were a significant part of the region and played a role in the lives of the [Israelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israelites). The study asserts that the Arab nation is an ancient and significant entity; however, it highlights that the Arabs lacked a collective awareness of their unity. They did not inscribe their identity as Arabs or assert exclusive ownership over specific territories.[^cite_note-140]



Map of Midian

[Magan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magan_\(civilization\)), [Midian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midian), and [ʿĀd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BF%C4%80d) are all ancient tribes or civilizations that are mentioned in Arabic literature and have roots in the Arabia. Magan ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): مِجَانُ, *_Majan_*), known for its production of copper and other metals, the region was an important trading center in ancient times and is mentioned in the [Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) as a place where [Musa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_in_Islam) ([Moses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses)) traveled during his lifetime.[^cite_note-The_Archeology_Fund-141][^cite_note-142] Midian ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): مَدْيَن, *_Madyan_*), on the other hand, was a region located in the northwestern part of the Arabia, the people of Midian are [mentioned in the Qur'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_characters_and_names_mentioned_in_the_Quran) as having worshiped idols and having been punished by God for their disobedience.[^cite_note-143][^cite_note-144] Moses also lived in Midian for a time, where he married and worked as a shepherd. ʿĀd ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): عَادَ, *_ʿĀd_*), as mentioned earlier, was an ancient tribe that lived in the southern Arabia, the tribe was known for its wealth, power, and advanced technology, but they were ultimately destroyed by a powerful windstorm as punishment for their disobedience to [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allah).[^cite_note-Brill1-145] ʿĀd is regarded as one of the original Arab tribes.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-Brill8-147] The historian [Herodotus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herodotus) provided extensive information about Arabia, describing the [spices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spice), [terrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrain), [folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore), [trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade), [clothing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clothing), and [weapons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapon) of the Arabs. In his third book, he mentioned the Arabs as a force to be reckoned with in the north of the Arabian Peninsula just before the [first Achaemenid conquest of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Achaemenid_conquest_of_Egypt). Other Greek and Latin authors who wrote about Arabia include [Theophrastus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophrastus), [Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo), [Diodorus Siculus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diodorus_Siculus), and [Pliny the Elder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pliny_the_Elder). The Jewish historian [Flavius Josephus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josephus) wrote about the Arabs and their king, mentioning their relationship with [Cleopatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleopatra), the queen of Egypt. The tribute paid by the Arab king to Cleopatra was collected by [Herod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herod_the_Great), the king of the Jews, but the Arab king later became slow in his payments and refused to pay without further deductions.[^cite_note-148] [Geshem the Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geshem_the_Arabian) was an Arab man who opposed [Nehemiah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nehemiah) in the Hebrew Bible ([Neh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_of_Nehemiah). [2\:19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nehemiah_2\:19), [6\:1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nehemiah_6\:1)). He was likely the chief of the Arab tribe "Gushamu" and have been a powerful ruler with influence stretching from northern Arabia to Judah. The Arabs and the [Samaritans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samaritans) made efforts to hinder Nehemiah's rebuilding of the [walls of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walls_of_Jerusalem).[^cite_note-149][^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151]



Arabia Petraea or simply Arabia existed from the 2nd century onwards. Including regions in Jordan, Palestine, Israel, the Sinai Peninsula, and the northwestern Arabian Peninsula



[Al-Khazneh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Khazneh) in [Petra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petra), capital of the [Nabataean Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_Kingdom), built as a mausoleum to Nabataean King [Aretas IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aretas_IV) in the first century AD

The term "[Saracens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saracens)" was a term used in the early centuries, both in [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) and [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) writings, to refer to the "Arabs" who lived in and near what was designated by the [Romans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) as [Arabia Petraea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Petraea) (Levant) and [Arabia Deserta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Deserta) (Arabia).[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-Lionheart-153] The Christians of [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberia) used the term [*Moor*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moors) to describe all the Arabs and Muslims of that time. Arabs of Medina referred to the nomadic tribes of the deserts as the A'raab, and considered themselves sedentary, but were aware of their close racial bonds. [Hagarenes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagarenes) is a term widely used by early [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language), Greek, and [Armenian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_language) to describe the early Arab conquerors of Mesopotamia, Syria and Egypt, refers to the descendants of Hagar, who bore a son named Ishmael to Abraham in the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible), in which the Hagarenes are referred to as "Ishmaelites" or "Arabs."[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-Sefaria-Hebrew-Bible-Genesis-25-12-18-155] The [Arab conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_conquests) in the 7th century was a sudden and dramatic conquest led by Arab armies, which quickly conquered much of the Middle East, North Africa, and Spain. It was a significant moment for [Islam](6037917.md), which saw itself as the successor of Judaism and Christianity.[^cite_note-156]

### Antiquity

Main articles: [Pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia), [Dilmun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilmun), [Gerrha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrha), [Thamud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thamud), [Qedarites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qedarites), and [Lihyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lihyan)



Receipt for garments sent by boat to Dilmun in the 1st year of [Ibbi-Sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibbi-Sin)'s rule, circa 2028 BCE.

[^cite_note-157]

[^cite_note-158]

Limited local historical coverage of these civilizations means that archaeological evidence, foreign accounts and Arab oral traditions are largely relied on to reconstruct this period. Prominent civilizations at the time included, [Dilmun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilmun) civilization was an important trading centre[^cite_note-hoj-159] which at the height of its power controlled the [Arabian Gulf](24761.md) trading routes.[^cite_note-hoj-159] The [Sumerians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumer) regarded Dilmun as [holy land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_space).[^cite_note-160] Dilmun is regarded as one of the oldest ancient civilizations in the [Middle East](19323.md).[^cite_note-161][^cite_note-uns-162] which arose around the 4th millennium BCE and lasted to 538 BCE. [Gerrha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerrha) was an ancient city of [Eastern Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Arabia), on the west side of the Gulf, Gerrha was the center of an Arab kingdom from approximately 650 BCE to circa CE 300. [Thamud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thamud), which arose around the 1st millennium BCE and lasted to about 300 CE. From the beginning of the first millennium BCE, [Proto-Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Arabic_language), or [Ancient North Arabian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_North_Arabian), texts give a clearer picture of the Arabs' emergence. The earliest are written in variants of [epigraphic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epigraph_\(literature\)) south Arabian *[musnad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Arabian_alphabet)* script, including the 8th century BCE [Hasaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ahsa_Oasis) inscriptions of eastern Saudi Arabia, the [Thamudic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thamudic) texts found throughout the Arabian Peninsula and [Sinai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula).



Map of the Qedarite Kingdom in the 5th century B.C

The [Qedarites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qedarites) were a largely nomadic ancient Arab tribal confederation centred in the [Wādī Sirḥān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Sirhan) in the [Syrian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Desert). They were known for their [nomadic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomad) lifestyle and for their role in the caravan trade that linked the Arabian Peninsula with the [Mediterranean](19006.md) world. The Qedarites gradually expanded their territory over the course of the 8th and 7th centuries BCE, and by the 6th century BCE, they had consolidated into a kingdom that covered a large area in northern Arabia, southern Palestine, and the [Sinai Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinai_Peninsula). The Qedarites were influential in the [ancient Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Near_East), and their kingdom played a significant role in the political and economic affairs of the region for several centuries.[^cite_note-163]



The Queen of Sheba

[Sheba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheba) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): سَبَأٌ *Saba*) is kingdom mentioned in the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible) ([Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament)) and the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran), though Sabaean was a South Arabian language and not an Arabic one. Sheba features in [Jewish](15624.md), [Muslim](6037917.md), and [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian) traditions, whose lineage goes back to [Qahtan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qahtanite) [son of](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben) [Hud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hud_\(prophet\)), one of the ancestors of the Arabs,[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-qref|27|6-93|b=y-165][^cite_note-qref|34|15-18|b=y-166] Sheba was mentioned in Assyrian inscriptions and in the writings of [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) and [Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) writers.[^cite_note-British_Museum_–_The_kingdoms_of_ancient_South_Arabia-167] One of the ancient written references that also spoke of Sheba is the Old Testament, which stated that the people of Sheba supplied Syria and Egypt with incense, especially frankincense, and exported gold and precious stones to them.[^cite_note-168]



A bronze statue of Dhamar Ali Yahbur II, a [Himyarite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite) king who reigned in late 3rd or early 4th century CE. Displayed in the [Sana'a National Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sana'a_National_Museum).

[Sabaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabaeans) are mentioned several times in the [Hebrew Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_Bible). In the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran),[^cite_note-Brannon2002-169] they are described as either *Sabaʾ* (سَبَأ, not to be confused with *[Ṣābiʾ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabians)*, صَابِئ),[^cite_note-qref|27|6-93|b=y-165][^cite_note-qref|34|15-18|b=y-166] or as *Qawm [Tubbaʿ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tubba')* (Arabic: قَوْم تُبَّع, lit. 'People of Tubbaʿ').[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171] They were known for their prosperous trade and agricultural economy, which was based on the cultivation of frankincense and myrrh. These highly valued aromatic resins were exported to Egypt, Greece, and [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), making the Sabaeans wealthy and powerful, they also traded in spices, textiles, and other luxury goods. The [Maʾrib Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marib_Dam) was one of the greatest engineering achievements of the ancient world, and it provided water for the city of [Maʾrib](3604484.md) and the surrounding agricultural lands.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-Kitchen-173][^cite_note-British_Museum_–_The_kingdoms_of_ancient_South_Arabia-167]

[Lihyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lihyan) also called Dadān or Dedan was a powerful and highly organized ancient Arab kingdom that played a vital cultural and economic role in the north-western region of the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) and used [Dadanitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dadanitic) language.[^cite_note-Britannica_Lihyan2-174] The Lihyanites were known for their advanced organization and governance, and they played a significant role in the cultural and economic life of the region. The kingdom was centered around the city of Dedan (modern-day [Al Ula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-'Ula)), and it controlled a large territory that extended from [Yathrib](36636.md) in the south to parts of the Levant in the north.[^cite_note-175][^cite_note-Britannica_Lihyan2-174] The Arab genealogies consider the Banu Lihyan to be [Ishmaelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ishmaelites), and used Dadanitic language.[^cite_note-176]

&#x20;The [Kingdom of Ma'in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minaeans) was an ancient Arab kingdom with a hereditary monarchy system and a focus on [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture) and [trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade).[^cite_note-177] Proposed dates range from the 15th century BCE to the 1st century CE Its history has been recorded through inscriptions and classical Greek and Roman books, although the exact start and end dates of the kingdom are still debated. The Ma'in people had a local governance system with councils called "Mazood," and each city had its own temple that housed one or more gods. They also adopted the [Phoenician alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenician_alphabet) and used it to write their language. The kingdom eventually fell to the [Arab Sabaean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sabaeans) people.[^cite_note-178][^cite_note-179]



Hellenistic-style Qatabānian sculpture depicting the Moon as a baby boy riding a lion representing the Sun.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Beek1997-180]

Qataban was an ancient kingdom located in the [South Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Arabia), which existed from the early 1st millennium BCE till the late 1st or 2nd centuries CE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2009578-181][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2009578-181][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitchen2001123-182] It developed into a centralized state in the 6th century BCE with two co-kings ruling poles.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2009578-181][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyland200248-183] Qataban expanded its territory, including the conquest of Ma'in and successful campaigns against the Sabaeans.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitchen2001123-182][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Beek1997-180][^cite_note-FOOTNOTESchiettecatte2017-184] It challenged the supremacy of the Sabaeans in the region and waged a successful war against Hadramawt in the 3rd century BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Beek1997-180][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyland200246-185] Qataban's power declined in the following centuries, leading to its annexation by Hadramawt and [Ḥimyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite_Kingdom) in the 1st century CE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyland200242-186][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKitchen2001123-182][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Beek1997-180][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2009578-181][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyland200247-187][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Beek1997-180]

The [Kingdom of Hadhramaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hadhramaut) it was known for its rich [cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_heritage), as well as its strategic location along important [trade routes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_route) that connected the [Middle East](19323.md), [South Asia](21566765.md), and [East Africa](260987.md).[^cite_note-188] The Kingdom was established around the 3rd century BCE, and it reached its peak during the 2nd century CE, when it controlled much of the southern Arabian Peninsula. The kingdom was known for its impressive [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture), particularly its distinctive towers, which were used as watchtowers, defensive structures, and homes for wealthy families.[^cite_note-189] The people of Hadhramaut were skilled in agriculture, especially in growing frankincense and myrrh. They had a strong maritime culture and traded with India, East Africa, and Southeast Asia.[^cite_note-190] Although the kingdom declined in the 4th century, Hadhramaut remained a cultural and economic center. Its legacy can still be seen today.[^cite_note-191]



1st century coin of the [Himyarite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite) Kingdom, southern coast of the [Arabian peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_peninsula).

The ancient [Kingdom of Awsān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Awsan) (8th–7th century BCE) was indeed one of the most important small kingdoms of [South Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Arabia), and its capital Ḥajar Yaḥirr was a significant center of trade and commerce in the ancient world. The destruction of the city in the 7th century BCE by the king and Mukarrib of Saba' Karab El Watar is a significant event in the history of South Arabia. The victory of the Sabaeans over Awsān is also a testament to the military might and strategic prowess of the Sabaeans, who were one of the most powerful and influential kingdoms in the region.[^cite_note-192]

The [Himyarite Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite_Kingdom) or Himyar, was an ancient kingdom that existed from around the 2nd century BCE to the 6th century CE. It was centered in the [city of Zafar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zafar,_Yemen), which is located in present-day Yemen. The Himyarites were an Arab people who spoke a [South Arabian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_South_Arabian) and were known for their prowess in trade and seafaring,[^cite_note-CP1-193] they controlled the [southern part of Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Arabia) and had a prosperous economy based on agriculture, commerce, and maritime trade, they were skilled in irrigation and terracing, which allowed them to cultivate crops in the arid environment. The Himyarites converted to [Judaism](15624.md) in the 4th century CE, and their rulers became known as the "Kings of the Jews", this conversion was likely influenced by their trade connections with the Jewish communities of the Red Sea region and the Levant, however, the Himyarites also tolerated other religions, including [Christianity](5211.md) and the local pagan religions.[^cite_note-CP1-193]

#### Classical antiquity

Main articles: [Nabataeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataeans), [Palmyra](46709309.md), [Palmyrene Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Empire), [Iturea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iturea), [Osroene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osroene), [Kingdom of Hatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hatra), [Arbayistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbayistan), [Adiabene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiabene), and [Emesene dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emesene_dynasty)



Pavarotti

Nabataean Kingdom spanned from southern Jordan to Damascus, including the Tihamah coastal plain and Hejaz region. (above) and Palmyrene Empire extended from Ancyra, central Anatolia, to Upper Egypt. (below)

The [Nabataeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataeans) were nomadic Arabs who settled in a territory centred around their capital of Petra in what is now Jordan.[^cite_note-194][^cite_note-\:02-195] Their early inscriptions were in [Aramaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aramaic), but gradually switched to Arabic, and since they had writing, it was they who made the first inscriptions in Arabic. The [Nabataean alphabet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_alphabet) was adopted by Arabs to the south, and evolved into modern Arabic script around the 4th century. This is attested by [Safaitic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safaitic) inscriptions (beginning in the 1st century BCE) and the many Arabic personal names in [Nabataean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean) inscriptions. From about the 2nd century BCE, a few inscriptions from [Qaryat al-Faw](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qaryat_al-Faw) reveal a dialect no longer considered *proto-Arabic*, but *pre-classical Arabic*. Five [Syriac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syriac_language) inscriptions mentioning Arabs have been found at [Sumatar Harabesi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumatar_Harabesi), one of which dates to the 2nd century CE.[^cite_note-196][^cite_note-197]



Queen [Zenobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenobia), c. 240 – c. 274 CE) was a third-century queen of the [Palmyrene Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Empire) in [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(region\)). One of several ancient female rulers in antiquity of Arab origin.

Arabs are first recorded in [Palmyra](46709309.md) in the late first millennium BCE.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA278_278\]-198] The soldiers of the sheikh Zabdibel, who aided the Seleucids in the battle of Raphia (217 BCE), were described as Arabs; Zabdibel and his men were not actually identified as Palmyrenes in the texts, but the name "Zabdibel" is a Palmyrene name leading to the conclusion that the sheikh hailed from Palmyra.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBryce2014\[httpsbooksgooglecombooksidXno9AgAAQBAJpgPA359_359\]-199] After the [Battle of Edessa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Edessa) in 260 CE. Valerian's capture by the Sassanian king [Shapur I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapur_I) was a significant blow to Rome, and it left the empire vulnerable to further attacks. [Zenobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zenobia) was able to capture most of the Near East, including Egypt and parts of Asia Minor. However, their empire was short-lived, as [Aurelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurelian) was able to defeat the Palmyrenes and recover the lost territories. The Palmyrenes were helped by their Arab allies, but Aurelian was also able to leverage his own alliances to defeat Zenobia and her army. Ultimately, the Palmyrene Empire lasted only a few years, but it had a significant impact on the history of the Roman Empire and the Near East.

Most scholars identify the [Itureans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iturea) as an Arab people who inhabited the region of Iturea,[^cite_note-200][^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203] emerged as a prominent power in the region after the decline of the Seleucid Empire in the 2nd century BCE, from their base around [Mount Lebanon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Lebanon) and the [Beqaa Valley](2162385.md), they came to dominate vast stretches of [Syrian territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(region\)),[^cite_note-204] and appear to have penetrated into northern parts of [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)) as far as the [Galilee](12639.md).[^cite_note-Berndt_Schaller_14922-89] [Tanukhids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanukhids) were an [Arab tribal confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia) that lived in the central and eastern Arabian Peninsula during the late ancient and early medieval periods. As mentioned earlier, they were a branch of the [Rabi'ah tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabi'a_ibn_Nizar), which was one of the largest Arab tribes in the pre-Islamic period. They were known for their military prowess and played a significant role in the early Islamic period, fighting in battles against the Byzantine and Sassanian empires and contributing to the expansion of the Arab empire.[^cite_note-205]



Map of the kingdoms of Osroene, Hatra, and Adiabene in Mesopotamia in 200 CE

&#x20;The [Osroene Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osroene), also known as the [Abgarids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abgarid_dynasty),[^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207][^cite_note-208] were in possession of the city of [Edessa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edessa) in the [ancient Near East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Near_East) for a significant period of time. Edessa was located in the region of Osroene, which was an ancient kingdom that existed from the 2nd century BCE to the 3rd century CE. They established a dynasty known as the Abgarids, which ruled Edessa for several centuries. The most famous ruler of the [dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abgarid_dynasty) was [Abgar V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abgar_V), who is said to have corresponded with [Jesus Christ](1095706.md) and is believed to have converted to [Christianity](5211.md).[^cite_note-209] The Abgarids played an important role in the early history of Christianity in the region, and Edessa became a center of Christian learning and [scholarship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_studies).[^cite_note-210] The [Kingdom of Hatra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hatra) was an ancient city located in the region of [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), it was founded in the 2nd or 3rd century BCE and flourished as a major center of trade and culture during the [Parthian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parthian_Empire). The rulers of Hatra were known as the Arsacid dynasty, which was a branch of the Parthian ruling family. However, in the 2nd century CE, the Arab tribe of [Banu Tanukh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanukhids) seized control of [Hatra](200437.md) and established their own dynasty. The Arab rulers of Hatra assumed the title of "malka," which means king in Arabic, and they often referred to themselves as the "King of the Arabs."[^cite_note-Hatra_and_the_Parthian_Commonwealth-211]



The Kingdom of Emesa

The Osroeni and Hatrans were part of several Arab groups or communities in upper Mesopotamia, which also included the Arabs of [Adiabene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adiabene) which was an ancient [kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy) in northern [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia), its chief city was [Arbela](24534207.md) (*Arba-ilu*), where Mar Uqba had a school, or the neighboring Hazzah, by which name the later Arabs also called Arbela.[^cite_note-212][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEKia201654-213] This Arab presence in upper Mesopotamia was acknowledged by the [Sasanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_dynasty), who called the region [Arbayistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbayistan), meaning "land of the Arabs", is first attested as a province in the [Ka'ba-ye Zartosht inscription](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapur_I's_inscription_at_the_Ka'ba-ye_Zartosht) of the second Sasanian [King of Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Kings), [Shapur I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shapur_I) (r. 240–270),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrunner1983b750-214] which was erected in c. 262.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTERapp201428-215][^cite_note-oxfordreference.com2-91] The [Emesene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emesene_dynasty) were a dynasty of Arab priest-kings that ruled the city of [Emesa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emesa) (modern-day [Homs](77533.md), Syria) in the [Roman province of Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Syria) from the 1st century CE to the 3rd century CE. The dynasty is notable for producing a number of high priests of the god [El-Gabal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elagabalus), who were also influential in [Roman politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_institutions_of_ancient_Rome) and culture. The first ruler of the Emesene dynasty was [Sampsiceramus I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampsiceramus_I), who came to power in 64 CE. He was succeeded by his son, [Iamblichus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iamblichus), who was followed by his own son, [Sampsiceramus II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampsiceramus_II). Under Sampsiceramus II, Emesa became a client kingdom of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), and the dynasty became more closely tied to Roman political and cultural traditions.[^cite_note-216]

#### Late antiquity

Further information: [Tanukh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanukh), [Salihids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salihids), [Lakhmid kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmid_kingdom), [Kingdom of Kinda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kinda), and [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids)



Map of the [Ghassanid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanid)



Map of [Salihids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salihids)



Map of the [Tanukhid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanukhid)

&#x20;The [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids), [Lakhmids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmids) and [Kindites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kindites) were the last major migration of pre-Islamic Arabs out of Yemen to the north. The Ghassanids increased the Semitic presence in then-Hellenized [Syria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syria_\(Byzantine_province\)), the majority of Semites were Aramaic peoples. They mainly settled in the [Hauran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hauran) region and spread to modern [Lebanon](17771.md), [Palestine](241405.md) and [Jordan](7515964.md). Greeks and Romans referred to all the nomadic population of the desert in the Near East as Arabi. The Romans called Yemen "[Arabia Felix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Felix)".[^cite_note-217] The Romans called the vassal nomadic states within the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) *[Arabia Petraea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Petraea)*, after the city of [Petra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petra), and called unconquered deserts bordering the empire to the south and east [Arabia Magna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Magna).&#x20;



Lakhmid kingdom

The [Lakhmids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmids) as a dynasty inherited their power from the [Tanukhids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanukh), the mid Tigris region around their capital [Al-Hira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hira). They ended up allying with the [Sassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) against the Ghassanids and the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire). The Lakhmids contested control of the Central Arabian tribes with the Kindites with the Lakhmids eventually destroying the [Kingdom of Kinda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kinda) in 540 after the fall of their main ally [Himyar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyar). The [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_people) Sassanids dissolved the Lakhmid dynasty in 602, being under puppet kings, then under their direct control.[^cite_note-218] The Kindites migrated from Yemen along with the Ghassanids and Lakhmids, but were turned back in Bahrain by the Abdul Qais [Rabi'a](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabi%60ah) tribe. They returned to Yemen and allied themselves with the Himyarites who installed them as a vassal kingdom that ruled Central Arabia from "Qaryah Dhat Kahl" (the present-day called Qaryat al-Faw). They ruled much of the Northern/Central Arabian peninsula, until they were destroyed by the Lakhmid king [Al-Mundhir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mundhir_III_ibn_al-Nu'man), and his son ['Amr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/'Amr_III_ibn_al-Mundhir).

The [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids) were an Arab tribe in the Levant in the early third century. According to Arab genealogical tradition, they were considered a branch of the [Azd tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azd). They fought alongside the [Byzantines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) against the [Sasanians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) and Arab Lakhmids. Most Ghassanids were Christians, converting to [Christianity](5211.md) in the first few centuries, and some merged with Hellenized Christian communities. After the Muslim conquest of the Levant, few Ghassanids became Muslims, and most remained Christian and joined Melkite and Syriac communities within what is now Jordan, Palestine, Syria, and Lebanon.[^cite_note-219] The [Salihids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salihids) were Arab foederati in the 5th century, were ardent Christians, and their period is less documented than the preceding and succeeding periods due to a scarcity of sources. Most references to the Salihids in Arabic sources derive from the work of [Hisham ibn al-Kalbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hisham_ibn_al-Kalbi), with the [Tarikh of Ya'qubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarikh_al-Yaqubi) considered valuable for determining the Salihids' fall and the terms of their foedus with the Byzantines.[^cite_note-220]

### Middle Ages

Main articles: [Spread of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam) and [Early Muslim conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests)



The early Arab conquests by reign

&#x20;During the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), Arab civilization flourished and the Arabs made significant contributions to the fields of [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science), [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics), [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy), and [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature), with the rise of great cities like [Baghdad](4492.md), [Cairo](6293.md), and [Cordoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba,_Spain), they became centers of learning, attracting scholars, scientists, and intellectuals.[^cite_note-Wenner_1980_59–79-221][^cite_note-A_Golden_age_of_Arab_culture-222] Arabs forged many empires and dynasties, most notably, the Rashidun Empire, the Umayyad Empire, the Abbasid Empire, the Fatimid Empire, among others. These empires were characterized by their expansion, scientific achievements, and cultural flourishing, extended from [Spain](26667.md) to India.[^cite_note-Wenner_1980_59–79-221] The region was vibrant and dynamic during the Middle Ages and left a lasting impact on the world.[^cite_note-A_Golden_age_of_Arab_culture-222]



The [rise of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam) began when [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) and his followers migrated from [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca) to [Medina](36636.md) in an event known as the [Hijra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijrah). Muhammad spent the last ten years of his life engaged in a series of battles to establish and expand the Muslim community. From 622 to 632, he led the Muslims in a state of war against the Meccans.[^cite_note-Collins142-223] During this period, the Arabs conquered the region of [Basra](57580.md), and under the leadership of [Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar), they established a base and built a mosque there. Another conquest was [Midian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midian), but due to its harsh environment, the settlers eventually moved to [Kufa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kufa). Umar successfully defeated rebellions by various Arab tribes, bringing stability to the entire Arabian peninsula and unifying it. Under the leadership of [Uthman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uthman), the Arab empire expanded through the [conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_conquest_of_Persia), with the capture of Fars in 650 and parts of [Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorasan_province) in 651.[^cite_note-Cunliffe4212-224] The [conquest of Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_conquest_of_armenia) also began in the 640s. During this time, the Rashidun Empire extended its rule over the entire [Sassanid Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sasanian_Empire) and more than two-thirds of the [Eastern Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire). However, the reign of [Ali ibn Abi Talib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali), the fourth caliph, was marred by the [First Fitna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fitna), or the First Islamic Civil War, which lasted throughout his rule. After a peace treaty with [Hassan ibn Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hasan_ibn_Ali) and the suppression of early [Kharijite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kharijites) disturbances, [Muawiyah I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'awiya_I) became the Caliph.[^cite_note-Brown15-225] This marked a significant transition in leadership.[^cite_note-Cunliffe4212-224][^cite_note-Collins376-226]

#### Arab empires

##### Rashidun era (632–661)

Main article: [Rashidun Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate)

See also: [Succession to Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Succession_to_Muhammad), [Saqifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saqifa), [Election of Uthman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Election_of_Uthman), and [Assassination of Uthman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assassination_of_Uthman)

Further information: [First Fitna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Fitna), [Muslim conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia), [Muslim conquest of the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Levant), [Arab conquest of Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_conquest_of_Egypt), and [Muslim conquest of the Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Maghreb)

After the death of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) in 632, [Rashidun armies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_army) launched campaigns of conquest, establishing the [Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate), or Islamic Empire, one of the [largest empires in history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_empires). It was larger and lasted longer than the previous Arab empire [Tanukhids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanukhids) of [Queen Mawia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mavia_\(queen\)) or the Arab [Palmyrene Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyrene_Empire). The Rashidun state was a completely new state and unlike the Arab kingdoms of its century such as the [Himyarite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite), [Lakhmids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmids) or [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids).

During the Rashidun era, the Arab community expanded rapidly, conquering many territories and establishing a vast Arab empire, which is marked by the reign of the first four caliphs, or leaders, of the Arab community.[^cite_note-Understanding_Islam:_The_First_Ten_Steps-227] These caliphs are [Abu Bakr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_Bakr), [Umar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar), [Uthman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uthman) and [Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali), who are collectively known as the Rashidun, meaning "rightly guided." The Rashidun era is significant in Arab and Islamic history as it marks the beginning of the Arab empire and the [spread of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam) beyond the Arabian Peninsula. During this time, the Arab community faced numerous challenges, including internal divisions and external threats from neighboring empires.[^cite_note-Understanding_Islam:_The_First_Ten_Steps-227][^cite_note-Triana_159-228]

Under the leadership of Abu Bakr, the Arab community successfully quelled a [rebellion](513246.md) by some tribes who refused to pay [Zakat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zakat), or Islamic charity. During the reign of Umar ibn al-Khattab, the Arab empire expanded significantly, conquering territories such as Egypt, [Syria](7515849.md), and [Iraq](7515928.md). The reign of Uthman ibn Affan was marked by internal dissent and rebellion, which ultimately led to his assassination. Ali, the cousin and son-in-law of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad), succeeded Uthman as caliph but faced opposition from some members of the Islamic community who believed he was not rightfully appointed.[^cite_note-Understanding_Islam:_The_First_Ten_Steps-227] Despite these challenges, the Rashidun era is remembered as a time of great progress and achievement in Arab and Islamic history. The caliphs established a system of governance that emphasized [justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice) and equality for all members of the Islamic community. They also oversaw the compilation of the Quran into a single text and spread Arabic teachings and principles throughout the empire. The Rashidun era continues to be revered by Muslims worldwide as a period of exemplary leadership and guidance.[^cite_note-229]

##### Umayyad era (661–750 and 756–1031)

Main articles: [Umayyad dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_dynasty) and [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate)

Further information: [Muslim conquest of Armenia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Armenia), [Muslim conquest of the Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Maghreb), [Muslim conquest of the Iberian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Iberian_Peninsula), [Muslim conquest of Transoxiana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Transoxiana), and [Umayyad campaigns in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_campaigns_in_India)

See also: [Abbadid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbadid_dynasty), [Taifa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taifa), [Nasrid dynasty (Sistan)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasrid_dynasty_\(Sistan\)), [Zengid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zengid_dynasty), [Ikhshidid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikhshidid_dynasty), [Umayyad state of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_state_of_C%C3%B3rdoba), and [Al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus)

In 661, the Rashidun Caliphate fell into the hands of the [Umayyad dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Umayya) and [Damascus](8914.md) was established as the empire's capital. The Umayyads were proud of their Arab identity and sponsored the poetry and culture of pre-Islamic Arabia. They established garrison towns at [Ramla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramla), [Raqqa](858965.md), [Basra](57580.md), [Kufa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kufa), [Mosul](200475.md) and [Samarra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samarra), all of which developed into major cities.[^cite_note-Lunde-230] [Caliph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliph) [Abd al-Malik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Malik_ibn_Marwan) established Arabic as the Caliphate's official language in 686.[^cite_note-231] Caliph [Umar II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umar_II) strove to resolve the conflict when he came to power in 717, demanding that all Muslims be treated as equals, but his intended reforms did not take effect, as he died after only three years of rule. By now, discontent with the Umayyads swept the region and an uprising occurred in which the [Abbasids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) came to power and moved the capital to [Baghdad](4492.md).



The [Caliphate of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate_of_C%C3%B3rdoba) during the reign of [Abd al-Rahman III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Rahman_III). Receiving the Ambassador by [Dionisio Baixeras Verdaguer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dionisio_Baixeras_Verdaguer) 1885 CE.

Umayyads expanded their Empire westwards capturing North Africa from the Byzantines. Before the Arab conquest, North Africa was conquered or settled by various people including [Punics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punics), Vandals and Romans. After the [Abbasid Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Revolution), the Umayyads lost most of their territories with the exception of Iberia.

Their last holding became known as the [Emirate of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_C%C3%B3rdoba). It was not until the rule of the grandson of the founder of this new emirate that the state entered a new phase as the [Caliphate of Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate_of_C%C3%B3rdoba). This new state was characterized by an expansion of trade, culture and knowledge, and saw the construction of masterpieces of [al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus) architecture and the library of [Al-Hakam II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hakam_II) which housed over 400,000 volumes. With the collapse of the Umayyad state in 1031 CE, al-Andalus was divided into [small kingdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taifa).[^cite_note-232]

##### Abbasid era (750–1258 and 1261–1517)

Main articles: [Abbasid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) and [Abbasid revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_revolution)

Further information: [Anarchy at Samarra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anarchy_at_Samarra), [Siege of Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Baghdad), and [Mongol invasions of the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasions_of_the_Levant)



[Harun al-Rashid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harun_al-Rashid) ([r.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reign) 786–809) receiving a delegation sent by [Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne) at his court in Baghdad.

The Abbasids were the descendants of [Abbas ibn Abd al-Muttalib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Abbas_ibn_Abd_al-Muttalib), one of the youngest uncles of Muhammad and of the same [Banu Hashim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Hashim) clan. The Abbasids led a revolt against the Umayyads and defeated them in the [Battle of the Zab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_the_Zab) effectively ending their rule in all parts of the Empire with the exception of al-Andalus. In 762, the second Abbasid Caliph [al-Mansur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mansur) founded the city of [Baghdad](4492.md) and declared it the capital of the Caliphate. Unlike the Umayyads, the Abbasids had the support of non-Arab subjects.[^cite_note-Lunde-230] The [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age) was inaugurated by the middle of the 8th century by the ascension of the Abbasid Caliphate and the transfer of the capital from [Damascus](8914.md) to the newly founded city of Baghdad. The Abbasids were influenced by the [Quranic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) injunctions and [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith) such as "The ink of the scholar is more holy than the blood of martyrs" stressing the value of knowledge.



[Al-Mustansiriyya Madrasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustansiriyya_Madrasa) in Baghdad

During this period the Abbasid Empire became an intellectual centre for science, philosophy, medicine and education as the Abbasids championed the cause of knowledge and established the "[House of Wisdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Wisdom)" in Baghdad. Rival dynasties such as the [Fatimids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid) of [Egypt](8087628.md) and the [Umayyads](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad) of al-Andalus were also major intellectual centres with cities such as [Cairo](6293.md) and [Córdoba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba,_Spain) rivaling [Baghdad](4492.md).[^cite_note-Vartan-233] In the 13th-century, the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_Empire) [conquered Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Baghdad_\(1258\)) in 1258 and killed the Caliph [Al-Musta'sim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Musta'sim). Members of the Abbasid royal family escaped the massacre and resorted to Cairo, which had broken from the Abbasid rule two years earlier; the [Mamluk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate_\(Cairo\)) generals taking the political side of the kingdom while Abbasid Caliphs were engaged in civil activities and continued patronizing science, arts and literature.

##### Fatimid era (909–1171)

Main articles: [Fatimid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_dynasty) and [Fatimid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate)



Bulgarian emperor [Simeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simeon_I_of_Bulgaria) (left) sending envoys to Caliph al-Mahdi (right). 12th-century miniature from the *[Madrid Skylitzes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madrid_Skylitzes)*

The Fatimid caliphate was founded by [al-Mahdi Billah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_al-Mahdi_Billah), a descendant of [Fatimah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimah), the daughter of Muhammad, the Fatimid Caliphate was a [Shia](26961.md) that existed from 909 to 1171 CE. The empire was based in North Africa, with its capital in [Cairo](6293.md), and at its height, it controlled a vast territory that included parts of modern-day [Egypt](8087628.md), [Libya](17633.md), [Tunisia](30188.md), [Algeria](358.md), [Morocco](19291.md), [Syria](7515849.md), and [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)). The Fatimid state took shape among the [Kutama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kutama), in the West of the North African littoral, in Algeria, in 909 conquering [Raqqada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raqqada), the [Aghlabid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aghlabid) capital. In 921 the Fatimids established the Tunisian city of [Mahdia](1086128.md) as their new capital. In 948 they shifted their capital to [Al-Mansuriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mansouria,_Tunisia), near [Kairouan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kairouan) in Tunisia, and in 969 they conquered Egypt and established Cairo as the capital of their caliphate.



[Al-Azhar Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azhar_Mosque) in [Cairo, Egypt](6293.md) in the [historic Islamic core of the city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Cairo), Cairo was established as the new capital of the [Fatimid Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate) in 970

The Fatimids were known for their religious tolerance and intellectual achievements, they established a network of universities and libraries that became centers of learning in the [Islamic world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world). They also promoted the arts, architecture, and literature, which flourished under their patronage. One of the most notable achievements of the Fatimids was the construction of the [Al-Azhar Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azhar_Mosque) and [Al-Azhar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azhar_University) in Cairo. Founded in 970 CE, it is one of the oldest universities in the world and remains an important center of Islamic learning to this day. The Fatimids also had a significant impact on the development of [Islamic theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schools_of_Islamic_theology) and [jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh). They were known for their support of Shia Islam and their promotion of the [Ismaili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma'ilism) branch of Shia Islam. Despite their many achievements, the Fatimids faced numerous challenges during their reign. They were constantly at war with neighboring empires, including the Abbasid Caliphate and the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire). They also faced internal conflicts and rebellions, which weakened their empire over time. In 1171 CE, the Fatimid Caliphate was conquered by the [Ayyubid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayyubid_dynasty), led by [Saladin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saladin). Although the Fatimid dynasty came to an end, its legacy continued to influence Arab-Islamic culture and society for centuries to come.[^cite_note-imamreza.net-234]

##### Ottoman era (1517–1918)

Main articles: [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) and [Ottoman Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Caliphate)

Further information: [Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt), [Campaigns of the Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaigns_of_the_Arab_Revolt), and [Middle Eastern theatre of World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Eastern_theatre_of_World_War_I)



[Hussein bin Ali al-Hashimi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_bin_Ali,_King_of_Hejaz) (1854–1931) was a prominent Arab leader who served as the [Sharif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharifian_Solution) and [Emir of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharif_of_Mecca) from 1908 until 1917. He was a member of the [Hashemite dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hashemites), which claimed descent from [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad).

From 1517 to 1918, The Ottomans defeated the [Mamluk Sultanate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk_Sultanate_\(Cairo\)) in Cairo, and ended the Abbasid Caliphate in the battles of [Marj Dabiq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Marj_Dabiq) and [Ridaniya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Ridaniya). They entered the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) and Egypt as conquerors, and brought down the Abbasid caliphate after it lasted for many centuries. In 1911, Arab intellectuals and politicians from throughout the Levant formed al-Fatat ("the [Young Arab Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Young_Arab_Society)"), a small Arab nationalist club, in Paris. Its stated aim was "raising the level of the [Arab nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Nationalist_Movement) to the level of modern nations." In the first few years of its existence, al-Fatat called for greater autonomy within a unified Ottoman state rather than Arab independence from the empire. Al-Fatat hosted the [Arab Congress of 1913](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Congress_of_1913) in Paris, the purpose of which was to discuss desired reforms with other dissenting individuals from the Arab world.[^cite_note-235] However, as the Ottoman authorities cracked down on the organization's activities and members, al-Fatat went underground and demanded the complete independence and unity of the Arab provinces.[^cite_note-Choueiri166-236]



The region covered by the modus vivendi, as agreed in the 1916 Sykes–Picot Agreement

The [Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt) was a military uprising of Arab forces against the Ottoman Empire during World War I, began in 1916, led by [Sherif Hussein bin Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_bin_Ali,_King_of_Hejaz), the goal of the revolt was to gain independence for the Arab lands under Ottoman rule and to create a unified Arab state. The revolt was sparked by a number of factors, including the Arab desire for greater autonomy within the Ottoman Empire, resentment towards Ottoman policies, and the influence of Arab nationalist movements. The Arab Revolt was a significant factor in the eventual [defeat of the Ottoman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissolution_of_the_Ottoman_Empire). The revolt helped to weaken Ottoman military power and tie up Ottoman forces that could have been deployed elsewhere. It also helped to increase support for Arab independence and nationalism, which would have a lasting impact on the region in the years to come.[^cite_note-237][^cite_note-238] The Empire's defeat and the occupation of part of its territory by the [Allied Powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) in the [aftermath of World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aftermath_of_World_War_I), the [Sykes–Picot Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sykes%E2%80%93Picot_Agreement) had a significant impact on the Arab world and its people. The agreement divided the Arab territories of the Ottoman Empire into zones of control for France and Britain, ignoring the aspirations of the Arab people for independence and self-determination.[^cite_note-239]

### Renaissance

Main articles: [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age), [Islamic world contributions to Medieval Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world_contributions_to_Medieval_Europe), and [Arab Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Renaissance)
