Further information: [List of inventions in the medieval Islamic world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_inventions_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world) and [Arab Agricultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Agricultural_Revolution)













From top to bottom and left to right: [al-Zahrawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Zahrawi), [Ibn al-Nafis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Nafis), [Averroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Averroes), [Ibn al-Haytham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Haytham), [Muhammad al-Idrisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_al-Idrisi), [al-Kindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Kindi)

The Golden Age of Arab Civilization known as the "*[Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age)*", traditionally dated from the 8th century to the 13th century.[^cite_note-Saliba-240][^cite_note-King-241][^cite_note-Hassan-Decline-242] The period is traditionally said to have ended with the collapse of the Abbasid caliphate due to [Siege of Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Baghdad_\(1258\)) in 1258.[^cite_note-Tahir_Abbas-243] During this time, [Arab scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_pre-modern_Arab_scientists_and_scholars) made significant contributions to fields such as mathematics, astronomy, medicine, and philosophy. These advancements had a profound impact on European scholars during the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance).[^cite_note-244]

The Arabs shared its knowledge and ideas with [Europe](9239.md), including translations of Arabic texts.[^cite_note-245] These translations had a significant impact on [culture of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Europe), leading to the transformation of many philosophical disciplines in the [medieval Latin world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Latin). Additionally, the Arabs made original innovations in various fields, including the arts, [agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Agricultural_Revolution), [alchemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alchemy), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music), and [pottery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pottery), and [traditional star names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arabic_star_names) such as [Aldebaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldebaran), scientific terms like *[alchemy](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/alchemy)* (whence also *[chemistry](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/chemistry)*), *[algebra](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/algebra)*, *[algorithm](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/algorithm)*, etc. and names of commodities such as *[sugar](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/sugar)*, *[camphor](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/camphor)*, *[cotton](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/cotton)*, *[coffee](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/coffee)*, etc.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247][^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]

From the medieval scholars of the [Renaissance of the 12th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance_of_the_12th_century), who had focused on studying [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) and [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) works of natural sciences, philosophy, and mathematics, rather than on such cultural texts. Arab logician, most notably [Averroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Averroes), had inherited Greek ideas after they had invaded and conquered [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Egypt) and the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_the_Levant). Their translations and commentaries on these ideas worked their way through the Arab West into [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus) and [Sicily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Sicily), which became important centers for this transmission of ideas. From the 11th to the 13th century, many schools dedicated to the translation of philosophical and scientific works from [Classical Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Arabic) to [Medieval Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Latin) were established in Iberia, most notably the [Toledo School of Translators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toledo_School_of_Translators). This work of translation from Arab culture, though largely unplanned and disorganized, constituted one of the greatest transmissions of ideas in history.[^cite_note-250]

During the [Timurid Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timurid_Renaissance) spanning the late 14th, the 15th, and the early 16th centuries, there was a significant exchange of ideas, art, and knowledge between different cultures and civilizations. Arab scholars, artists, and intellectuals played a role in this cultural exchange, contributing to the overall intellectual atmosphere of the time. They participated in various fields, including literature, art, science, and philosophy.[^cite_note-251] In the late 19th and early 20th centuries, the [Arab Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Renaissance) was a cultural and intellectual movement that emerged. The term "Nahda" means "awakening" or "renaissance" in Arabic, and refers to a period of renewed interest in Arabic language, literature, and culture.[^cite_note-Sheehi2004-252][^cite_note-253][^cite_note-Pacini1998-254]

### Modern period

Main articles: [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), [Arab League](52625.md), [Charter of the Arab League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_of_the_Arab_League), [Arab Maghreb Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Maghreb_Union), and [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md)

See also: [Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism), [Pan-Arabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism), [Arab Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Union), and [Arab socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_socialism)

See also: [Arab Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Federation), [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic), [United Arab States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_States), [Federation of Arab Republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Arab_Republics), [Arab Islamic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Islamic_Republic), and [Union of Arab Republics (1972)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Arab_Republics_\(1972\))



A map of the Arab world, formally the **Arab homeland**; also known as the **Arab nation.**

The modern period in [Arab history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Arabs) refers to the time period from the late 19th century to the present day. During this time, the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) experienced significant [political](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics), [economic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy), and social changes. One of the most significant events of the modern period was the collapse of the Ottoman Empire, the end of Ottoman rule led to the emergence of new [nation-states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_state) in the Arab world.[^cite_note-255][^cite_note-256]

&#x20;Sharif Hussein was supposed, in the event of the success of the Arab revolution and the victory of the [Allies in World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I), to be able to establish an independent Arab state consisting of the Arabian Peninsula and the Fertile Crescent, including Iraq and the Levant. He aimed to become "King of the Arabs" in this state, however, the Arab revolution only succeeded in achieving some of its objectives, including the [independence of the Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hejaz) and the recognition of Sharif Hussein as its king by the Allies.[^cite_note-257]



A 1920 photograph of four prominent members of [The Pen League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahjar) literal meanings being "the [Arab diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_diaspora)"

[^cite_note-258]

&#x20;predecessors in the Nahda movement (or the "[Arab Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahda)"). (From left to right): [Nasib Arida](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasib_Arida), [Kahlil Gibran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahlil_Gibran), [Abd al-Masih Haddad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abd_al-Masih_Haddad), and [Mikhail Naimy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mikhail_Naimy).

[Arab nationalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_nationalism) emerged as a major movement in the early 20th century, with many Arab intellectuals, artists, and political leaders seeking to promote unity and independence for the Arab world.[^cite_note-Requiem-259] This movement gained momentum after [World War II](32927.md), leading to the formation of the Arab League and the creation of several new Arab states. [Pan-Arabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arabism) that emerged in the early 20th century and aimed to unite all Arabs into a single [nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation) or state. It emphasized on a shared [ancestry, culture, history, language and identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_identity) and sought to create a sense of pan-Arab identity and solidarity.[^cite_note-smith-260][^cite_note-Sela151-261]

The roots of pan-Arabism can be traced back to the [Arab Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahda) or Al-Nahda movement of the late 19th century, which saw a revival of Arab culture, literature, and intellectual thought. The movement emphasized the importance of Arab unity and the need to resist colonialism and foreign domination. One of the key figures in the development of pan-Arabism was the Egyptian statesman and intellectual, [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser), who led the [1952 revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1952_Egyptian_revolution) in Egypt and became the country's president in 1954. Nasser promoted pan-Arabism as a means of strengthening Arab solidarity and resisting Western imperialism. He also supported the idea of [Arab socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_socialism), which sought to combine pan-Arabism with socialist principles. Similar attempts were made by other [Arab leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_leaders_of_Middle_Eastern_and_North_African_states#Leaders_of_Arab_League_member_states), such as [Hafez al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafez_al-Assad), [Ahmed Hassan al-Bakr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Hassan_al-Bakr), [Faisal I of Iraq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_I_of_Iraq), [Muammar Gaddafi](53029.md), [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein), [Gaafar Nimeiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaafar_Nimeiry) and [Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat).[^cite_note-262]



The [flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_the_Arab_Revolt) of the [Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt) against the [Ottoman Empire](22278.md) is a prominent symbol of Arab nationalism. Its design and [colors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arab_colors) are the basis of many of the [Arab states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_states)' [flags](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arab_flags). The [Pan-Arab colors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-Arab_colors) are [black](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black), [white](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White), [green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green) and [red](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red). Individually, each of the four Pan-Arab colors were intended to represent a certain aspect of the Arabs and their history.

Many proposed unions aimed to create a [unified Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Union) entity that would promote cooperation and integration among Arab countries. However, the initiatives faced numerous challenges and obstacles, including political divisions, regional conflicts, and economic disparities.[^cite_note-feb1-263] The [United Arab Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Republic) (UAR) was a political union formed between Egypt and Syria in 1958, with the goal of creating a federal structure that would allow each member state to retain its identity and institutions. However, by 1961, Syria had withdrawn from the UAR due to political differences, and Egypt continued to call itself the UAR until 1971, when it became the [Arab Republic of Egypt](8087628.md). In the same year the UAR was formed, another proposed political union, the [Arab Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Federation), was established between [Jordan](7515964.md) and [Iraq](7515928.md), but it collapsed after only six months due to tensions with the UAR and the [14 July Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14_July_Revolution). A confederation called the [United Arab States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_States), which included the UAR and the [Mutawakkilite Kingdom of Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutawakkilite_Kingdom_of_Yemen), was also created in 1958 but dissolved in 1961.[^cite_note-Continuum-264] Later attempts to create a political and economic union among Arab countries included the [Federation of Arab Republics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federation_of_Arab_Republics), which was formed by Egypt, [Libya](17633.md), and Syria in the 1970s but dissolved after five years due to political and economic challenges. Muammar Gaddafi, the leader of Libya, also proposed the [Arab Islamic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Islamic_Republic) with Tunisia, aiming to include [Algeria](358.md) and [Morocco](19291.md),[^cite_note-265] instead the [Arab Maghreb Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Maghreb_Union) was formed in 1989.[^cite_note-266]

During the latter half of the 20th century, many Arab countries experienced political upheaval and conflicts, including, revolutions. The [Arab–Israeli conflict](7960202.md) remains a major issue in the region, and has resulted in ongoing tensions and periodic outbreaks of violence. In recent years, the Arab world has faced new challenges, including economic and social inequalities, demographic changes, and the impact of [globalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization).[^cite_note-267] The [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) was a series of pro-democracy uprisings and protests that swept across several countries in the Arab world in 2010 and 2011. The uprisings were sparked by a combination of political, economic, and social grievances and called for democratic reforms and an end to authoritarian rule. While the protests resulted in the downfall of some long-time authoritarian leaders, they also led to ongoing conflicts and political instability in other countries.[^cite_note-268]

## Identity

Further information: [Arab identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_identity)

Costumes of Arab men, fourth to sixth century

Costumes of Arab women, fourth to sixth century.

Artistic rendering of pre-Islamic costumes of Arab men and Arab women between fourth to sixth century

Arab identity is defined independently of [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion) identity, and pre-dates the [spread of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spread_of_Islam), with historically attested [Arab Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians) kingdoms and [Arab Jewish tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewish_tribes_of_Arabia). Today, however, most Arabs are Muslim, with a minority adhering to other faiths, largely [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_Middle_East), but also [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) and [Baháʼí](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith).[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270] Paternal descent has traditionally been considered the main source of affiliation in the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world) when it comes to membership into an [ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnicity) or [clan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clan).[^cite_note-271]

Arab identity is shaped by a range of factors, including ancestry, history, language, customs, [social construct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_construct)[^cite_note-272] and traditions.[^cite_note-273] Arab identity has been shaped by a rich history that includes the rise and fall of [empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire), [colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonization), and political turmoil. Despite the challenges faced by Arab communities, their shared cultural heritage has helped to maintain a sense of unity and pride in their identity.[^cite_note-Imagining_the_Arabs_:_Arab_identity_and_the_rise_of_Islam-274] Today, Arab identity continues to evolve as Arab communities navigate complex political, social, and economic landscapes. Despite this, the Arab identity remains an important aspect of the cultural and historical fabric of the Arab world, and continues to be celebrated and preserved by [communities around the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_diaspora).[^cite_note-275]

## Subgroups

Further information: [Tribes of Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia), [Arab migrations to the Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migrations_to_the_Maghreb), and [Arab migrations to the Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migrations_to_the_Levant)



The Yaman tribes, including the Banu Kalb, Ghassan, Judham and Tanukh, largely inhabited the districts of [Filastin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jund_Filastin), [al-Urdunn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jund_al-Urdunn) and [Hims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jund_Hims), while the Qays inhabited [al-Jazirah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_Mesopotamia), the [Byzantine frontier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Awasim) and [Qinnasrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jund_Qinnasrin).

Arab tribes are prevalent in the Arabian Peninsula, Mesopotamia, Levant, Egypt, Maghreb, the Sudan region and Horn Africa.[^cite_note-276][^cite_note-Imagining_the_Arabs_:_Arab_identity_and_the_rise_of_Islam-274][^cite_note-277]

The Arabs of the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant) have been traditionally divided into [Qays and Yaman tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qays_and_Yaman_tribes). The distinction between Qays and Yaman was based on tribal affiliations and geographic locations. The [Yaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yaman_\(tribal_group\)) is traditionally said to include [Banu Kalb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Kalb), [Kinda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinda_\(tribe\)), [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids), and [Lakhmids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmids).[^cite_note-278] The [Qays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qays) is said to be made up of tribes such as [Banu Kilab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Kilab) and [Banu Tamim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Tamim) among others.

There are also many Arab tribes indigenous to Mesopotamia (Iraq) and Iran, including from well before the [Muslim conquest of Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_conquest_of_Persia) in 633 CE.[^cite_note-IranicaDaniel2-279] The largest group of [Iranian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Arabs) are the [Khuzestani Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestani_Arabs), including [Banu Ka'b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Ka'b), [Bani Turuf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bani_Turuf) and the [Musha'sha'iyyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musha'sha'iyyah) sect. Smaller groups are the [Khamseh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khamseh) nomads in [Fars province](448049.md) and the [Khorasani Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khorasani_Arabs). As a result of the centuries-long [Arab migration to the Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migration_to_the_Maghreb), various Arab tribes (including [Banu Hilal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Hilal), [Banu Sulaym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Sulaym) and [Maqil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqil)) also settled in the Maghreb and formed the sub-tribes which exist to present-day. The Banu Hilal spent almost a century in [Egypt](8087628.md) before moving to [Libya](17633.md), [Tunisia](30188.md) and [Algeria](358.md), and another century later moved to [Morocco](19291.md).[^cite_note-280]

According to Arab traditions, tribes are divided into different divisions called Arab skulls, which are described in the traditional custom of strength, abundance, victory, and honor. A number of them branched out, which later became independent tribes (sub-tribes). The majority of Arab tribes are descended from these major tribes.[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282][^cite_note-المفصل_فى_تاريخ_العرب_قبل_الإسلام22-283][^cite_note-284][^cite_note-285]

They are:[^cite_note-المفصل_فى_تاريخ_العرب_قبل_الإسلام22-283]



A family tree depicting the descendants of the Banu Adnan.

- [Bakr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Bakr), has descendants in Arabia and [Iraq](7515928.md).

  [^cite_note-286]

- [Kinanah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kinana), has descendants in [Arabia](47858.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Egypt](8087628.md), [Sudan](27421.md), [Palestine](241405.md), [Tunisia](30188.md), [Morocco](19291.md), and [Syria](7515849.md).

  [^cite_note-Brill2-287]

  [^cite_note-288]

  [^cite_note-289]

  [^cite_note-290]

- [Azd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azd), has descendants in [Arabia](47858.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant), and [North Africa](21714.md).

## Geographic distribution

### Arab homeland

Further information: [Demographics of the Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_the_Arab_world) and [List of Arab League countries by population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arab_League_countries_by_population)



Countries with significant Arab population and descendants.

&#x20; Arab world

&#x20; + 5,000,000

&#x20; + 1,000,000

&#x20; + 100,000

The total number of Arabs living in the [Arab nations](52625.md) is estimated at 366 million[^cite_note-295] to 400 million.[^cite_note-296] The estimated number of Arabs in countries outside the Arab League is estimated at 17.5 million, yielding a total of close to 384 million.[^cite_note-World_Arabic_Language_Day-297] The Arab world stretches around 13,000,000 square kilometres (5,000,000 sq mi), from the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) in the west to the [Arabian Sea](2747.md) in the east and from the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) in the north to the [Horn of Africa](62142.md) and the [Indian Ocean](14580.md) in the southeast.&#x20;



Geographical distribution of Afroasiatic languages and [varieties of Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Arabic)

### Arab diaspora

Main article: [Arab diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_diaspora)

[Arab diaspora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_diaspora) refers to descendants of the [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_people) [immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emigration) who, voluntarily or as refugees, emigrated from their native lands in non-Arab countries, primarily in [East Africa](260987.md), [South America](26769.md), [Europe](9239.md), [North America](21139.md), Australia and parts of [South Asia](21566765.md), [Southeast Asia](28741.md), the [Caribbean](18956035.md), and [West Africa](67393.md). They reportedly number around 30 million.[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299] According to the [International Organization for Migration](36893.md), there are 13 million first-generation Arab migrants in the world, of which 5.8 million reside in Arab countries. Arab expatriates contribute to the circulation of financial and human capital in the region and thus significantly promote regional development. In 2009, Arab countries received a total of US\$35.1 billion in [remittance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remittance) in-flows and remittances sent to [Jordan](7515964.md), [Egypt](8087628.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md) from other Arab countries are 40 to 190 per cent higher than trade revenues between these and other Arab countries.[^cite_note-300] The 250,000 strong Lebanese community in West Africa is the largest non-African group in the region.[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302] Arab traders have long operated in Southeast Asia and along the East Africa's [Swahili coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swahili_coast). [Zanzibar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zanzibar) was once ruled by [Omani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Omanis) Arabs.[^cite_note-303] Most of the prominent [Indonesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Indonesians), [Malaysians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Malaysians), and [Singaporeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Singaporeans) of Arab descent are [Hadhrami people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadhrami_people) with origins in southern Arabia in the [Hadramaut](325752.md) coastal region.[^cite_note-304]

#### Europe

Main articles: [Arabs in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Europe), [Arabs in Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Austria), [Arabs in Bulgaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Bulgaria), [Arabs in Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Finland), [Arabs in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_France), [Arabs in Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Germany), [Arabs in Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Greece), [Arabs in Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Italy), [Arabs in the Netherlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_the_Netherlands), [Arabs in Romania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Romania), [Arabs in Serbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Serbia), [Arabs in Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Spain), [Arabs in Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Sweden), [Arabs in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Switzerland), [Arabs in Turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Turkey), [British Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Arabs), [Arabs in Malta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Malta), and [Arma people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arma_people)





[Alhambra](30543.md) is one of the most famous monuments of [Islamic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_architecture). (left) [Arab World Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_World_Institute), is an organisation founded in Paris in 1980 by France with 18 Arab countries (right)

There are millions of [Arabs living in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Europe), mostly concentrated in [France](5843419.md) (about 6,000,000 in 2005[^cite_note-variety.com-305]). Most [Arabs in France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_France) are from the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb) but some also come from the [Mashreq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mashreq) areas of the Arab world. Arabs in France form the second largest [ethnic group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_group) after [French people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_people).[^cite_note-economist.com2-306] In [Italy](14532.md), Arabs first arrived on the southern island of [Sicily](27619.md) in the 9th century. The largest modern societies on the island from the Arab world are Tunisians and Moroccans, who make up 10.9% and 8% respectively of the foreign population of Sicily, which in itself constitutes 3.9% of the island's total population.[^cite_note-307] The modern [Arab population of Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Spain) numbers 1,800,000,[^cite_note-308][^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310][^cite_note-311] and there have been Arabs in [Spain](26667.md) since the early 8th century when the [Muslim conquest of Hispania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_conquest_of_Hispania) created the state of Al-Andalus.[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313][^cite_note-314] In [Germany](11867.md) the Arab population numbers over 1,401,950.[^cite_note-statista2-315][^cite_note-cz-herborn.de2-316] in the United Kingdom between 366,769[^cite_note-Naba11a2-317] and 500,000,[^cite_note-318] and in [Greece](12108.md) between 250,000 and 750,000[^cite_note-Gre12-319]). In addition, Greece is home to people from Arab countries who have the status of refugees (e.g. [refugees of the Syrian civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_civil_war)).[^cite_note-Kniffka19952-320] In the [Netherlands](21148.md) 180,000,[^cite_note-auto2-40] and in [Denmark](76972.md) 121,000. Other countries are also home to Arab populations, including [Norway](21241.md), [Austria](26964606.md), [Bulgaria](3415.md), [Switzerland](26748.md), [North Macedonia](23564616.md), [Romania](25445.md) and [Serbia](29265.md).[^cite_note-Arab_migration_facts_and_figures2-321] As of late 2015, [Turkey](11125639.md) had a total population of 78.7 million, with [Syrian refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugees_of_the_Syrian_Civil_War) accounting for 3.1% of that figure based on conservative estimates. Demographics indicated that the country previously had 1,500,000[^cite_note-ChapinMetz2-322] to 2,000,000 Arab residents,\[^cite_note-[www.aljazeera.com2-10](http://www.aljazeera.com2-10/)] [Turkey's Arab population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Turkey) is now 4.5 to 5.1% of the total population, or approximately 4–5 million people.\[^cite_note-[www.aljazeera.com2-10\]\[^cite_note-www.washingtoninstitute.org2-323](http://www.aljazeera.com2-10]\[^cite_note-www.washingtoninstitute.org2-323)]

#### Americas

Main articles: [Arab immigration to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_immigration_to_the_United_States), [Arab Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Americans), [History of Middle Eastern people in Metro Detroit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Middle_Eastern_people_in_Metro_Detroit), [Arab Canadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Canadians), [Arab Brazilians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Brazilians), [Arab Mexicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Mexicans), [Arab Chileans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Chileans), [Arab Colombians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Colombians), [Arab Uruguayans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Uruguayans), [Arab Venezuelans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Venezuelans), [Arab Cubans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Cubans), [Arab Haitians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Haitians), [Arab immigration to Honduras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_immigration_to_Honduras), [Arab Argentines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Argentines), [Lebanese Paraguayans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Paraguayans), and [Arab Uruguayans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Uruguayans)

See also: [Arab American Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_American_Institute), [Arab American National Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_American_National_Museum), [Arab American Action Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_American_Action_Network), and [Arab American Book Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_American_Book_Award)





[Gigi Hadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gigi_Hadid) is a model and television personality, [Steve Jobs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_Jobs) was the co-founder, chairman, and CEO of Apple, [Shakira](6479315.md) is a singer and songwriter and [Salma Hayek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salma_Hayek) is an actress and film producer.

[Arab immigration to the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_immigration_to_the_United_States) began in significant numbers during the 1880s, and today, an estimated 2 million Americans trace their roots to an Arab background according to the Census Bureau.[^cite_note-American_Community_Survey-19][^cite_note-324] [Arab Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Americans) are found in every state, but more than two thirds of them live in just ten states, and one-third live in [Los Angeles](18110.md), [Detroit](8687.md), and New York City specifically.[^cite_note-aaiusa.org-325][^cite_note-326] Most Arab Americans were born in the US, and nearly 82% of US-based Arabs are citizens.[^cite_note-autogenerated42-327][^cite_note-aai.3cdn.net2-328][^cite_note-329][^cite_note-330]

Arab immigrants began to arrive in Canada in small numbers in 1882. Their [immigration](4599312.md) was relatively limited until 1945, after which time it increased progressively, particularly in the 1960s and thereafter.[^cite_note-331] According to the website "Who are [Arab Canadians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Canadians)", [Montreal](7954681.md), the Canadian city with the largest Arab population, has approximately 267,000 Arab inhabitants.[^cite_note-332]

[Latin America](18524.md) has the largest Arab population outside of the [Arab World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_World).[^cite_note-333] Latin America is home to anywhere from 17–25 to 30 million people of Arab descent, which is more than any other diaspora region in the world.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] The [Brazilian](33367309.md) and Lebanese governments claim there are 7 million [Brazilians of Lebanese descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_Brazilians).[^cite_note-itamaraty12-336][^cite_note-libano12-337] Also, the Brazilian government claims there are 4 million [Brazilians of Syrian descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Brazilians).[^cite_note-itamaraty12-336][^cite_note-IBGE2008-5][^cite_note-saudiaramcoworld12-338][^cite_note-dsbra2-339][^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341] Other large Arab communities includes [Argentina](18951905.md) (about 3,500,000[^cite_note-Fearab.org.ar-13][^cite_note-oni.escuelas.edu.ar2-342][^cite_note-343])

The interethnic marriage in the Arab community, regardless of religious affiliation, is very high; most community members have only one parent who has Arab ethnicity.[^cite_note-344] [Colombia](5222.md) (over 3,200,000[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-346][^cite_note-347]), [Venezuela](32374.md) (over 1,600,000),[^cite_note-thedailybeast.com-26][^cite_note-348] [Mexico](3966054.md) (over 1,100,000),[^cite_note-349] [Chile](5489.md) (over 800,000),[^cite_note-Chile:_Oriente_próximo,_800.000,_Palestinos,_500.000-31][^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351][^cite_note-select.nytimes.com2-352] and [Central America](6121.md), particularly [El Salvador](9356.md), and [Honduras](13394.md) (between 150,000 and 200,000).[^cite_note-The_Arabs_of_Honduras2-353][^cite_note-Adnkronos.com-33][^cite_note-Laventana.casa.cult.cu-34] [Arab Haitians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Haitians) (257,000[^cite_note-354]) a large number of whom live in the [capital](57391.md) are more often than not, concentrated in financial areas where the majority of them establish businesses.[^cite_note-al-shorfa.com2-355]

#### Caucasus

Main article: [Arabs in the Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_the_Caucasus)



Georgia and the Caucasus in 1060, during the final decline of the emirate

In 1728, a Russian officer described a group of Arab nomads who populated the [Caspian](19653787.md) shores of [Mughan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughan_plain) (in present-day [Azerbaijan](746.md)).[^cite_note-Genko2-356] It is believed that these groups migrated to the [South Caucasus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Caucasus) in the 16th century.[^cite_note-zelkina2-357] The 1888 edition of [Encyclopædia Britannica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A6dia_Britannica) also mentioned a certain number of Arabs populating the [Baku Governorate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baku_Governorate) of the [Russian Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Empire).[^cite_note-Baynesp5142-358] They retained an [Arabic dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirvani_Arabic) at least into the mid-19th century,[^cite_note-Bakik2-359] there are nearly 30 settlements still holding the name *Arab* (for example, [Arabgadim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C6%8Fr%C9%99bq%C9%99dim), [Arabojaghy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C6%8Fr%C9%99boca%C4%9F%C4%B1), [Arab-Yengija](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C6%8Fr%C9%99byengic%C9%99), etc.). From the time of the Arab conquest of the South Caucasus, continuous small-scale Arab migration from various parts of the Arab world occurred in [Dagestan](751099.md). The majority of these lived in the village of Darvag, to the north-west of [Derbent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Derbent). The latest of these accounts dates to the 1930s.[^cite_note-zelkina2-357] Most Arab communities in southern Dagestan underwent linguistic [Turkicisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkification), thus nowadays Darvag is a majority-[Azeri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azerbaijanis) village.[^cite_note-Sefer2-360][^cite_note-Wrum2-361]

#### Central, South, East and Southeast Asia

Main articles: [Arabs in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_India), [Arab Indonesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Indonesians), [Central Asian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asian_Arabs), [Iranian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_Arabs), [Arabs in Khorasan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Khorasan), [Khuzestani Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khuzestani_Arabs), [Arabs in Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Japan), [Arab Malaysians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Malaysians), [Arabs in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_the_Philippines), [Arab Singaporeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Singaporeans), and [Sri Lankan Moors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_Moors)

According to the *History of Ibn Khaldun*, the Arabs that were once in [Central Asia](6742.md) have been either killed or have fled the Tatar invasion of the region.[^cite_note-Khaldun2-362] However, today many people in Central Asia identify as Arabs. Most [Arabs of Central Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Asian_Arabic) are fully integrated into local populations, and sometimes call themselves the same as locals (for example, [Tajiks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C4%81jik_people), [Uzbeks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uzbeks)) but they use special titles to show their Arab origin such as [Sayyid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sayyid), [Khoja](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khoja_\(Turkestan\)) or [Siddiqui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siddiqui).[^cite_note-Owensp1842-363]



The mosque is built at the spot where the first Arab traders landed and subsequently settled in the area.

[^cite_note-364]

There are only two communities in India which claim Arab descent, the [Chaush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaush) of the [Deccan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deccan_Plateau) region and the [Chavuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_\(Gujarat\)) of [Gujarat](53707.md).[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] These groups are largely descended from [Hadhrami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadhrami_people) migrants who settled in these two regions in the 18th century. However, neither community still speaks Arabic, although the Chaush have seen re-immigration to [Eastern Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Arabia) and thus a re-adoption of Arabic.[^cite_note-367] In [South Asia](21566765.md), where Arab ancestry is considered prestigious, some communities have origin myths that claim Arab ancestry. Several communities following the [Shafi'i madhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shafi%CA%BDi_school) (in contrast to other [South Asian Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_South_Asia) who follow the [Hanafi madhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanafi)) claim descent from Arab traders like the [Konkani Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konkani_Muslims) of the [Konkan region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Konkan), the [Mappilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mappilla) of [Kerala](4349459.md), and the [Labbai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labbai) and [Marakkar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marakkar) of [Tamil Nadu](29918.md) and a few Christian groups in India that claim and have Arab roots are situated in the state of Kerala.[^cite_note-368] South Asian [Iraqi biradri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iraqi_Biradari) may have records of their ancestors who migrated from Iraq in historical documents. The [Sri Lankan Moors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_Moors) are the third largest ethnic group in [Sri Lanka](26750.md), constituting 9.2% of the country's total population.[^cite_note-369] Some sources trace the ancestry of the Sri Lankan [Moors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moors) to Arab traders who settled in Sri Lanka at some time between the 8th and 15th centuries.[^cite_note-TheSundayTimes2-370][^cite_note-Anthropos2-371][^cite_note-Islamic_Studies2-372] There are about 118,866 [Arab-Indonesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab-Indonesians)[^cite_note-373] of [Hadrami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadhrami_people) descent in the 2010 Indonesian census.[^cite_note-374]

#### Sub-Saharan Africa

Main articles: [Afro-Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Arabs), [Baggara Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baggara_Arabs), [Ghanaian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghanaian_Arabs), [Arabs in Ivory Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabs_in_Ivory_Coast), [Lebanese people in Senegal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_people_in_Senegal), [Lebanese people in Sierra Leone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_people_in_Sierra_Leone), and [Diffa Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diffa_Arabs)



Map of the Baggara belt

[Afro-Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Arabs) are individuals and groups from [Africa](5334607.md) who are of partial Arab descent. Most Afro-Arabs inhabit the [Swahili Coast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swahili_coast) in the [African Great Lakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Great_Lakes) region, although some can also be found in parts of the Arab world.[^cite_note-Romero2-375][^cite_note-Gunnar2-376] Large numbers of Arabs migrated to [West Africa](67393.md), particularly [Côte d'Ivoire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B4te_d'Ivoire) (home to over 100,000 Lebanese),[^cite_note-377] [Senegal](27278.md) (roughly 30,000 Lebanese),[^cite_note-378] [Sierra Leone](27308.md) (roughly 10,000 Lebanese today; about 30,000 prior to the outbreak of [civil war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sierra_Leone_Civil_War) in 1991), [Liberia](17791.md), and [Nigeria](21383.md).[^cite_note-379] Since the end of the civil war in 2002, [Lebanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lebanese_people) traders have become re-established in Sierra Leone.[^cite_note-380][^cite_note-381][^cite_note-382] The Arabs of Chad occupy northern Cameroon and Nigeria (where they are sometimes known as Shuwa), and extend as a belt across Chad and into Sudan, where they are called the [Baggara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baggara) grouping of [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) ethnic groups inhabiting the portion of Africa's [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel). There are 171,000 in [Cameroon](5447.md), 150,000 in [Niger](21373.md)[^cite_note-383]), and 107,000 in the [Central African Republic](5478.md).[^cite_note-384]

## Religion

Main articles: [Religion in pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia), [Arab Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Muslims), [Arab Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians), [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze), [Arab Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Jews), and [Baháʼí Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith)

Arabs are mostly Muslims with a [Sunni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) majority and a [Shia](26961.md) minority, one exception being the [Ibadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadis), who predominate in [Oman](22316.md).[^cite_note-ahmadi2-385] [Arab Christians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians) generally follow [Eastern Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Churches) such as the [Greek Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Orthodox) and [Greek Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_Catholic) churches, though a minority of [Protestant Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Church) followers also exists.[^cite_note-Christi2-386] There are also Arab communities consisting of [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) and [Baháʼís](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bah%C3%A1%CA%BC%C3%AD_Faith).[^cite_note-387][^cite_note-388] Historically, there were also sizeable populations of [Arab Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Jews) around the Arab World.

[Before the coming of Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia), most Arabs followed a pagan religion with a number of deities, including [Hubal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubal),[^cite_note-Hubal2-389] [Wadd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadd), [Allāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-l%C4%81t),[^cite_note-autogenerated12-390] [Manat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man%C4%81t), and [Uzza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Uzza). A few individuals, the *[hanifs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanif)*, had apparently rejected [polytheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytheism) in favor of [monotheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monotheism) unaffiliated with any particular religion. Some tribes had converted to Christianity or Judaism. The most prominent Arab Christian kingdoms were the [Ghassanid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanid) and [Lakhmid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmid) kingdoms.[^cite_note-Marib2-391] When the [Himyarite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Himyarite) king converted to [Judaism](15624.md) in the late 4th century,[^cite_note-Hima2-392] the elites of the other prominent Arab kingdom, the [Kindites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kindites), being Himyirite vassals, apparently also converted (at least partly). With the expansion of Islam, polytheistic Arabs were rapidly [Islamized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamization), and polytheistic traditions gradually disappeared.[^cite_note-MSNU2-393][^cite_note-EncyRe2-394]



[Kaaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaba) is the holiest place in Islam, [Masjid al-Haram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masjid_al-Haram) in [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca)



The [Church of the Nativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_Nativity) in [Bethlehem](4312.md), State of Palestine

Today, Sunni Islam dominates in most areas, vastly so in Levant, North Africa, West Africa and the Horn of Africa. Shia Islam is dominant in [Bahrain](18933277.md) and southern [Iraq](7515928.md) while northern Iraq is mostly Sunni. Substantial Shia populations exist in [Lebanon](17771.md), [Yemen](350939.md), [Kuwait](7515890.md), [Saudi Arabia](349303.md),[^cite_note-Shia2-395] northern [Syria](7515849.md) and Al-Batinah Region in [Oman](22316.md). There are small numbers of [Ibadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibadi) and [non-denominational Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-denominational_Muslims) too.[^cite_note-ahmadi2-385] The [Druze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Druze) community is concentrated in Levant.[^cite_note-396]

Christianity had a prominent presence In [pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia) among several Arab communities, including the [Bahrani people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahrani_people) of [Eastern Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Arabia), the [Christian community of Najran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_community_of_Najran), in parts of [Yemen](350939.md#Ancient_history), and among certain northern Arabian tribes such as the [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids), [Lakhmids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmids), [Taghlib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taghlib), [Banu Amela](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Amela), [Banu Judham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banu_Judham), [Tanukhids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanukhids) and [Tayy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tayy). In the early Christian centuries, Arabia was sometimes known as *Arabia heretica*, due to its being "well known as a breeding-ground for heterodox interpretations of Christianity."[^cite_note-397] Christians make up 5.5% of the population of Western Asia and North Africa.[^cite_note-Pacini2-398] In Lebanon, Christians number about 40.5% of the population.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook:_Lebanon2-399] In Syria, Christians make up 10% of the population.[^cite_note-CIA_World_Factbook:_Syria2-400] [Christians in Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_Christians) make up 8% and 0.7% of the populations, respectively.[^cite_note-FactbookGazaStrip2-401][^cite_note-FactbookWestBank2-402] In Egypt, Christians number about 10% of the population. In Iraq, Christians constitute 0.1% of the population.[^cite_note-403]

In Israel, Arab Christians constitute 2.1% (roughly 9% of the Arab population).[^cite_note-WFIS2-404] Arab Christians make up 8% of the population of [Jordan](7515964.md).[^cite_note-WFJord2-405] Most [North](21139.md) and [South American](26769.md) Arabs are Christian,[^cite_note-AAIUSA2-406] so are about half of the Arabs in Australia who come particularly from Lebanon, Syria and Palestine. One well known member of this religious and ethnic community is [Saint Abo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abo_of_Tiflis), martyr and the patron saint of [Tbilisi](42298.md), [Georgia](48768.md).[^cite_note-407] Arab Christians also live in holy Christian cities such as [Nazareth](21702.md), [Bethlehem](4312.md) and the [Christian Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Quarter) of the [Old City of Jerusalem](302972.md) and many other villages with holy Christian sites.

## Culture

Main article: [Arab culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_culture)



Dancing girls at Cairo

Arab culture is shaped by a long and rich history that spans thousands of years, from the [Atlantic Ocean](698.md) in the west to the [Arabian Sea](2747.md) in the east, and from the [Mediterranean Sea](19006.md) in the north to the Horn of Africa and the Indian Ocean in the southeast. The various religions the Arabs have adopted throughout [their history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Arabs) and the various empires and kingdoms that have ruled and took lead of the Arabic civilization have contributed to the [ethnogenesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnogenesis) and formation of modern Arab culture. [Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Language), [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature), [gastronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastronomy), [art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art), [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music), [spirituality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirituality), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy) and [mysticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysticism) are all part of the cultural heritage of the Arabs.[^cite_note-408]

### Language

Main article: [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic)



[Arabic calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_calligraphy) is the artistic practice of writing Arabic script in a decorative and stylized manner.

[Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) is a [Semitic language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semitic_language) of the [Afro-Asiatic family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afroasiatic_languages).[^cite_note-409] The first evidence for the emergence of the language appears in military accounts from 853 BCE. Today it has developed widely used as a *[lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)* for more than 500 million people. It is also a [liturgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy#Islam) language for 1.7 billion [Muslims](19541.md).[^cite_note-pewmuslim422-410][^cite_note-411] Arabic is one of six [official languages of the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_languages_of_the_United_Nations),[^cite_note-412] and is revered in [Islam](6037917.md) as the language of the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran).[^cite_note-pewmuslim422-410][^cite_note-413]

Arabic has two main registers. [Classical Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Arabic) is the form of the [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) language used in literary texts from [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) and Abbasid times (7th to 9th centuries). It is based on the medieval dialects of [Arab tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia). [Modern Standard Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Standard_Arabic) (MSA) is the direct descendant used today throughout the Arab world in writing and in formal speaking, for example, prepared speeches, some radio broadcasts, and non-entertainment content,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBin-Muqbil200614-414] while the [lexis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lexis_\(linguistics\)) and [stylistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stylistics_\(linguistics\)) of Modern Standard Arabic are different from Classical Arabic. There are also various regional dialects of colloquial spoken Arabic that both vary greatly from both each other and from the formal written and spoken forms of Arabic.[^cite_note-415]

### Mythology

Main article: [Arabian mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_mythology)



[Aladdin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aladdin) from the *[One Thousand and One Nights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Thousand_and_One_Nights)*

[Arabic mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_mythology) comprises the ancient beliefs of the Arabs. Prior to Islam the Kaaba of Mecca was covered in symbols representing the myriad demons, djinn, demigods, or simply tribal gods and other assorted deities which represented the polytheistic culture of pre-Islamic. It has been inferred from this plurality an exceptionally broad context in which mythology could flourish.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417]

The most popular beasts and demons of Arabian mythology are [Bahamut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bahamut), [Dandan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dandan), [Falak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falak_\(Arabian_legend\)), [Ghoul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghoul), [Hinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinn_\(mythology\)), [Jinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinn), [Karkadann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karkadann), [Marid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marid), [Nasnas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasnas), [Qareen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qareen), [Roc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roc_\(mythology\)), [Shadhavar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadhavar), [Werehyena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Werehyena) and other assorted creatures which represented the profoundly [polytheistic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytheistic) environment of pre-Islamic.[^cite_note-418]

The most prominent symbol of Arabian mythology is the [Jinn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jinn) or genie.[^cite_note-419] Jinns are supernatural beings that can be good or evil.[^cite_note-420][^cite_note-421] They are not purely spiritual, but are also physical in nature, being able to interact in a tactile manner with people and objects and likewise be acted upon. The *jinn*, humans, and [angels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_view_of_angels) make up the known [sapient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sapience) creations of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Islam).[^cite_note-422]

[Ghouls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghoul) also feature in the mythology as a [monster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monster) or [evil spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evil_spirit) associated with graveyards and consuming human flesh.[^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424] In Arabic folklore, ghouls belonged to a diabolic class of jinn and were said to be the offspring of Iblīs, the prince of darkness in Islam. They were capable of constantly changing form, but always retained [donkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donkey)'s hooves.[^cite_note-425]

### Literature

Main article: [Arabic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_literature)



Manuscript from the *Diwan* of [Al-Mutanabbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Mutanabbi)

The [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran), the main [holy book](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_text) of [Islam](6037917.md), had a significant influence on the Arabic language, and marked the beginning of Arabic literature. Muslims believe it was transcribed in the Arabic dialect of the [Quraysh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quraysh), the tribe of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad).[^cite_note-Glen_Van_Brummelen-2013-426][^cite_note-427] As Islam spread, the Quran had the effect of unifying and standardizing Arabic.[^cite_note-Glen_Van_Brummelen-2013-426]

Not only is the Quran the first work of any significant length written in the language, but it also has a far more complicated structure than the earlier literary works with its 114 *[suwar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surah)* (chapters) which contain 6,236 *[ayat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ayat)* (verses). It contains [injunctions](39343.md), [narratives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrative), [homilies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homilies), [parables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parable), direct addresses from God, instructions and even comments on how the Quran will be received and understood. It is also admired for its layers of metaphor as well as its clarity, a feature which is mentioned in [An-Nahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An-Nahl), the 16th surah.

[Al-Jahiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Jahiz) (born 776, in [Basra](57580.md) – December 868/January 869) was an Arab prose writer and author of works of literature, [Mu'tazili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mu'tazili) theology, and politico-religious polemics. A leading scholar in the Abbasid Caliphate, his canon includes two hundred books on various subjects, including [Arabic grammar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_grammar), [zoology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoology), poetry, lexicography, and [rhetoric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetoric). Of his writings, only thirty books survive. Al-Jāḥiẓ was also one of the first Arabian writers to suggest a complete overhaul of the language's grammatical system, though this would not be undertaken until his fellow linguist [Ibn Maḍāʾ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Ma%E1%B8%8D%C4%81%CA%BE) took up the matter two hundred years later.[^cite_note-428]

There is a small remnant of [pre-Islamic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_poetry), but Arabic literature predominantly emerges in the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages), during the [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age).[^cite_note-429] [Imru' al-Qais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imru'_al-Qais) was a king and poet in the 6th century, he was the last king of [Kindite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Kinda). He is among the finest Arabic poetry to date, as well sometimes considered the father of [Arabic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_poetry).[^cite_note-430] *[Kitab al-Aghani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kitab_al-Aghani)* by [Abul-Faraj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu_al-Faraj_al-Isfahani) was called by the 14th-century historian [Ibn Khaldun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Khaldun) the register of the Arabs.[^cite_note-431] [Literary Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Standard_Arabic) is derived from [Classical Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Arabic), based on the language of the Quran as it was analyzed by [Arabic grammarians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_grammarians) beginning in the 8th century.[^cite_note-432]



[Kahlil Gibran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahlil_Gibran) was a writer, poet and visual artist; he is best known as the author of *[The Prophet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Prophet_\(book\))*, has since become one of the [best-selling books](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_best-selling_books) of all time, having been [translated into more than 100 languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Translations_of_The_Prophet)

A large portion of Arabic literature before the 20th century is in the form of [poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_poetry), and even prose from this period is either filled with snippets of poetry or is in the form of *[saj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saj_\(literature\))* or rhymed prose.[^cite_note-433] The *[ghazal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghazal)* or love poem had a long history being at times tender and chaste and at other times rather explicit.[^cite_note-434] In the [Sufi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sufi) tradition the love poem would take on a wider, [mystical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysticism) and [religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion) importance.

[Arabic epic literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_epic_literature) was much less common than poetry, and presumably originates in oral tradition, written down from the 14th century or so. [Maqama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqama) or [rhymed prose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhymed_prose) is intermediate between poetry and [prose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prose), and also between fiction and non-fiction.[^cite_note-435] Maqama was an incredibly popular form of Arabic literature, being one of the few forms which continued to be written during the decline of Arabic in the 17th and 18th centuries.[^cite_note-436]

Arabic literature and [culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_culture) declined significantly after the 13th century, to the benefit of [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_Turkish_language) and [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_literature). A modern revival took place beginning in the 19th century, alongside [resistance against Ottoman rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_awakenings_under_the_Ottoman_Empire). The literary revival is known as *[al-Nahda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Nahda)* in Arabic, and was centered in [Egypt](8087628.md) and [Lebanon](17771.md). Two distinct trends can be found in the *nahda* period of revival.[^cite_note-437]

The first was a neo-classical movement which sought to rediscover the literary traditions of the past, and was influenced by traditional literary genres—such as the *[maqama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqama)*—and works like *[One Thousand and One Nights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_Thousand_and_One_Nights)*. In contrast, a modernist movement began by translating Western modernist works—primarily novels—into Arabic.[^cite_note-438] A tradition of [modern Arabic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_Arabic_poetry) was established by writers such as [Francis Marrash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Marrash), [Ahmad Shawqi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmad_Shawqi) and [Hafiz Ibrahim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hafiz_Ibrahim). Iraqi poet [Badr Shakir al-Sayyab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badr_Shakir_al-Sayyab) is considered to be the originator of free verse in [Arabic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_poetry).[^cite_note-439][^cite_note-440][^cite_note-441]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Arab cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_cuisine)



Plate of [Mezes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meze) in [Petra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petra), Jordan

[Arab cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_cuisine) is largely divided into [Khaleeji cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khaleeji_cuisine), [Levantine cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levantine_cuisine) and [Maghrebi cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghrebi_cuisine).[^cite_note-442] Arab cuisine has influenced other cuisines various cultures, including [Ottoman](22278.md), [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_peoples), and [Andalusian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andalusian_Spanish).

It is characterized by a variety of herbs and spices, including [cumin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cumin), [coriander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coriander), [cinnamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinnamon), [sumac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumac), [za'atar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Za'atar), [cardamom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardamom), [mint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mentha), [saffron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saffron), [sesame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sesame), [thyme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thyme) [turmeric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turmeric) and [parsley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parsley).[^cite_note-Food_and_recipes2-443][^cite_note-444] Arab cuisine is also known for its sweets and desserts, such as [Knafeh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Knafeh), [Baklava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baklava), [Halva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halva), and [Qatayef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatayef). [Arabic coffee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_coffee), or qahwa, is a traditional drink that is served with dates.

### Art

Main articles: [Arabic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_art), [Nabataean art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_art), [Arabic miniature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_miniature), and [Arabesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabesque)



















(1st row) Various examples of early Umayyad paintings in Qusayr 'Amra. (2nd row) Examples of Abbasid Figural paintings from Samarra. (3rd row) Examples of Fatimid art.

[Arabic art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_art) has taken various forms, including, among other things, [jewelry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewelry), [textiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile) and [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Architecture).[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446] Arabic script has also traditionally been heavily embellished with often colorful [Arabic calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_calligraphy), with one notable and widely used example being [Kufic script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kufic_script).[^cite_note-447] Arabic miniatures ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic): الْمُنَمْنَمَات الْعَرَبِيَّة, *Al-Munamnamāt al-ʿArabīyah*) are small [paintings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Painting) on [paper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper), usually book or manuscript illustrations but also sometimes separate artworks that occupy entire pages. The earliest example dates from around 690 CE, with a flourishing of the art from between 1000 and 1200 CE in the Abbasid caliphate. The art form went through several stages of evolution while witnessing the fall and rise of several [Arab caliphates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate).





Arabic miniature

Arab miniaturists got totally assimilated and subsequently disappeared due to the [Ottoman](22278.md) occupation of the Arab world. Nearly all forms of Islamic miniatures ([Persian miniatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_miniature), [Ottoman miniatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_miniature) and [Mughal miniatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mughal_miniature)) owe their existences to Arabic miniatures, as Arab patrons were the first to demand the production of illuminated manuscripts in the Caliphate, it was not until the 14th century that the artistic skill reached the non-Arab regions of the Caliphate.[^cite_note-448][^cite_note-DavidCollection2-449][^cite_note-MMA19332-450][^cite_note-451][^cite_note-452]

Despite the considerable changes in Arabic miniature style and technique, even during their last decades, the early [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) Arab influence could still be noticed. Arabic miniature artists include [Ismail al-Jazari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_al-Jazari), who illustrated his own *Book of Knowledge of Ingenious Mechanical Devices.*[^cite_note-Jazari2-453]

&#x20;The Abbasid artist, [Yahya Al-Wasiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_ibn_Mahmud_al-Wasiti), who probably lived in [Baghdad](4492.md) in the late Abbasid era (12th to 13th-centuries), was one of the pre-eminent exponents of the Baghdad school. In the period 1236–1237, he transcribed and illustrated the book *Maqamat* (also known as the *Assemblies* or the *Sessions*), a series of anecdotes of social satire written by [Al-Hariri of Basra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Hariri_of_Basra).[^cite_note-454] The narrative concerns the travels of a middle-aged man as he uses his charm and eloquence to swindle his way across the Arabic world.[^cite_note-455]



Arabesque pattern behind hunters on [ivory plaque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivory_carving), 11th–12th century, Egypt

With most surviving Arabic [manuscripts](19167.md) in western museums,[^cite_note-456] Arabic miniatures occupy very little space in modern Arab culture.[^cite_note-457] [Arabesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabesque) is a form of artistic decoration consisting of "surface decorations based on rhythmic linear patterns of scrolling and interlacing foliage, tendrils" or plain lines,[^cite_note-458] often combined with other elements. Another definition is "Foliate ornament, typically using leaves, derived from stylised [half-palmettes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-palmette), which were combined with spiralling stems".[^cite_note-459] It usually consists of a single design which can be 'tiled' or seamlessly repeated as many times as desired.[^cite_note-RobinsonIllustrated2-460][^cite_note-461]

### Architecture

Further information: [Nabataean architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_architecture) and [Islamic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_architecture)

The Arab world is home to around 8%[^cite_note-462] of [UNESCO](21786641.md) [World Heritage Sites](44940.md) ([List of World Heritage Sites in Arab states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_World_Heritage_Sites_in_Arab_states)). The oldest examples of architecture include those of [pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia),[^cite_note-\:05222-463] as well as [Nabataean architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_architecture) that developed in the ancient [kingdom of the Nabataeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataean_Kingdom), a nomadic Arab tribe that controlled a significant portion of the [Middle East](19323.md) from the 4th century BCE to the 2nd century CE.[^cite_note-464][^cite_note-465] The Nabataeans were known for their skill in carving out elaborate buildings, tombs, and other structures from the sandstone cliffs of the region. One of the most famous examples of Nabataean architecture is the city of [Petra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petra), which is located in modern-day [Jordan](7515964.md), was the capital of the Nabataean kingdom and is renowned for its impressive rock-cut architecture.[^cite_note-466]

Prior to the start of the [Arab conquests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests), Arab tribal client states, the [Lakhmids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lakhmid_kingdom) and [Ghassanids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ghassanids), were located on the borders of the Sassanid and Byzantine empires and were exposed to the cultural and architectural influences of both.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahîd1995a401–403-467][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEFloodNecipoğlu201758-468] They most likely played a significant role in transmitting and adapting the architectural traditions of these two empires to the later Arab Islamic dynasties.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEShahîd1995a391,_402-469][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEEttinghausenGrabarJenkins-Madina20014–5-470]



The [Dome of the Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dome_of_the_Rock) located in Jerusalem



[Alhambra](30543.md) is a palace and fortress complex located in Granada, Andalusia, Spain.

The Arab empire expanded rapidly, and with it, came a diverse range of architectural influences. One of the most notable architectural achievements of the Arab Empire is the [Great Mosque of Damascus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Mosque) in Syria, which was built in the early 8th century, was constructed on the site of a Christian basilica and incorporated elements of Byzantine and Roman architecture, such as arches, columns, and intricate mosaics. Another important architectural is the [Al-Aqsa Mosque](65759.md) in [Jerusalem](16043.md), which was built in the late 7th century. The mosque features an impressive dome and a large prayer hall, as well as intricate geometric patterns and calligraphy on the walls.[^cite_note-471][^cite_note-Krautheimer2-472]

### Music

Main article: [Arabic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_music)



[Umm Kulthum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Kulthum) was an Arab singer, [songwriter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Songwriter), and film actress (1920s–1970s). She has been named among the "200 Greatest Singers of All Time".

[^cite_note-473]

[Arabic music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_music), while independent and flourishing in the 2010s, has a long history of interaction with many other regional musical styles and genres. It is an amalgam of the music of the Arab people in the Arabian Peninsula and the music of all the peoples that make up the Arab world today.[^cite_note-474] Pre-Islamic Arab music was similar to that of Ancient Middle Eastern music. Most historians agree that there existed distinct forms of music in the [Arabian peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_peninsula) in the pre-Islamic period between the 5th and 7th century CE. [Arab poets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_poetry) of that "Jahili poets", meaning "the poets of the period of ignorance"—used to recite poems with a high notes.[^cite_note-Jahili2-475] It was believed that [Jinns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genie) revealed poems to poets and music to musicians.[^cite_note-Jahili2-475] By the 11th century, Islamic Iberia had become a center for the manufacture of instruments. These goods spread gradually throughout [France](5843419.md), influencing French [troubadours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troubadour), and eventually reaching the rest of [Europe](9239.md). The English words [lute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lute), [rebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebec), and [naker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqareh) are derived from Arabic [oud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oud), [rabab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebab), and [naqareh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqareh).[^cite_note-476][^cite_note-477]

A number of [musical instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_instrument) used in [classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_music) are believed to have been derived from Arabic musical instruments: the [lute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lute) was derived from the *[Oud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oud)*, the [rebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rebec) (ancestor of [violin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violin)) from the *[Maghreb rebab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb_rebab)*, the [guitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guitar) from *qitara*, which in turn was derived from the Persian [Tar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tar_\(lute\)), [naker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqareh) from *[naqareh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Naqareh)*, [adufe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adufe) from *[al-duff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daf)*, [alboka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alboka) from *al-buq*, *anafil* from *[al-nafir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nafir_\(trumpet\))*, exabeba from *al-shabbaba* ([flute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flute)), atabal ([bass drum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bass_drum)) from *al-tabl*, atambal from *al-tinbal*,[^cite_note-478] the [balaban](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balaban_\(instrument\)), the [castanet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castanet) from *kasatan*, [sonajas de azófar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuna_\(music\)) from *sunuj al-sufr*, the [conical bore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bore_\(wind_instruments\)) [wind instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_instrument),[^cite_note-479] the xelami from the *sulami* or *[fistula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fistula)* (flute or [musical pipe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organ_pipe)),[^cite_note-480] the [shawm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shawm) and [dulzaina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dulzaina) from the [reed instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reed_\(instrument\)) *zamr* and *[al-zurna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurna)*,[^cite_note-481] the [gaita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galician_gaita) from the *[ghaita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhaita)*, [rackett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rackett) from *iraqya* or *iraqiyya*,[^cite_note-482] [geige](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Violin) (violin) from *ghichak*,[^cite_note-483] and the [theorbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theorbo) from the *tarab*.[^cite_note-484]

During the 1950s and the 1960s, Arabic music began to take on a more Western tone – artists [Umm Kulthum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umm_Kulthum), [Abdel Halim Hafez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdel_Halim_Hafez), and [Shadia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shadia) along with composers [Mohamed Abd al-Wahab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Abdel_Wahab) and [Baligh Hamdi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baligh_Hamdi) pioneered the use of western instruments in Egyptian music. By the 1970s several other singers had followed suit and a strand of [Arabic pop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_pop) was born. Arabic pop usually consists of Western styled songs with Arabic instruments and lyrics. Melodies are often a mix between Eastern and Western. Beginning in the mid-1980s, [Lydia Canaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lydia_Canaan), musical [pioneer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovator) widely regarded as the first rock star of the [Middle East](19323.md)[^cite_note-Daily_Star_Rock_Hall2-485][^cite_note-486]

### Spirituality



Bas-relief: [Nemesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nemesis), [al-Lat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Lat) and the dedicator. [Palmyrene](46709309.md), 2nd–3rd century CE.

[Arab polytheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia) was the dominant religion in [pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia). [Gods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deity) and [goddesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goddess), including [Hubal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubal) and the goddesses [al-Lāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Lat), [Al-'Uzzá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-'Uzz%C3%A1) and [Manāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man%C4%81t), were worshipped at local shrines, such as the [Kaaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaba) in [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca), whilst Arabs in the south, in what is today's Yemen, worshipped various gods, some of which represented the Sun or Moon. Different theories [have been proposed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_pre-Islamic_Arabia#Mecca) regarding the role of [Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allah) in Meccan religion.[^cite_note-Robinson2-487][^cite_note-Peters2-488][^cite_note-Peterson20072-489] Many of the physical descriptions of the pre-Islamic gods are traced to [idols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_image), especially near the Kaaba, which is said to have contained up to 360 of them.[^cite_note-armstrong2-490] Until about the fourth century, almost all Arabs practised polytheistic religions.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEHoyland2002139-491] Although significant [Jewish](15624.md) and Christian minorities developed, [polytheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polytheism) remained the dominant belief system in pre-Islamic Arabia.[^cite_note-Nicolle2-492]
