The religious beliefs and practices of the nomadic [bedouin](61770.md) were distinct from those of the settled tribes of towns such as [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca).[^cite_note-Aslan62-493] Nomadic religious belief systems and practices are believed to have included [fetishism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fetishism), [totemism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totemism) and [veneration of the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_the_dead) but were connected principally with immediate concerns and problems and did not consider larger [philosophical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy) questions such as the afterlife.[^cite_note-Aslan62-493] Settled urban Arabs, on the other hand, are thought to have believed in a more complex [pantheon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pantheon_\(religion\)) of deities.[^cite_note-Aslan62-493] While the Meccans and the other settled inhabitants of the [Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz) worshipped their gods at permanent shrines in towns and oases, the bedouin practised their religion on the move.[^cite_note-Peters1052-494]

Most notable Arab gods and goddesses: ['Amm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/'Amm), [A'ra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A'ra), [Abgal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abgal_\(god\)), [Allah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allah#Pre-Islamic_Arabians), [Al-Lat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Lat), [Al-Qaum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Qaum), [Almaqah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almaqah), [Anbay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anbay), [ʿAṯtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BFA%E1%B9%AFtar), [Basamum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basamum), [Dhu l-Khalasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demolition_of_Dhul_Khalasa), [Dushara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dushara), [Haukim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haukim), [Hubal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hubal), [Isāf and Nā'ila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Is%C4%81f_and_N%C4%81'ila), [Manaf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manaf_\(deity\)), [Manāt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man%C4%81t), [Nasr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasr_\(deity\)), [Nuha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuha_\(deity\)), [Quzah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quzah), [Ruda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruda_\(deity\)), [Sa'd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sa'd_\(deity\)), [Shams, Samas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shams_\(goddess\)), [Syn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin_\(mythology\)), [Suwa'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suwa'), [Ta'lab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ta'lab), [Theandrios](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theandrios), [al-'Uzzá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-'Uzz%C3%A1), [Wadd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadd), [Ya'uq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ya'uq), [Yaghūth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yagh%C5%ABth), [Yatha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yatha), [Aglibol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aglibol), [Astarte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astarte), [Atargatis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atargatis), [Baalshamin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baalshamin), [Bēl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bel_\(mythology\)), [Bes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bes), [Ēl, Ilāh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El_\(deity\)), [Inanna/Ishtar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inanna), [Malakbel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malakbel), [Nabū, Nebo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabu), [Nergal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nergal), [Yarhibol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yarhibol).

### Philosophy

Main article: [Islamic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_philosophy)





Ibn Rushd (left), known in the west as [Averroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Averroes), was a philosopher that influenced the rise of secular thought in Western Europe, while [Ibn Khaldun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Khaldun) (right) was a sociologist, philosopher, and historian widely acknowledged to be one of the greatest social scientists of the Middle Ages.

The philosophical thought in the Arab world is heavily influenced by Arabic philosophy. Schools of Arabic/Islamic thought include [Avicennism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicennism) and [Averroism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Averroism). The first great Arab thinker in the Islamic tradition is widely regarded to be [al-Kindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Kindi) (801–873 A.D.), a [Neo-Platonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Platonic) philosopher, [mathematician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematician) and [scientist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientist) who lived in [Kufa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kufa) and [Baghdad](4492.md) (modern day [Iraq](7515928.md)). After being appointed by the [Abbasid Caliphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphs) to translate [Greek](42056.md) scientific and [philosophical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophical) texts into [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic), he wrote a number of original treatises of his own on a range of subjects, from [metaphysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics) and [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethic) to [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematic) and [pharmacology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmacology).[^cite_note-495]

Much of his [philosophical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy) output focuses on [theological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theology) subjects such as the nature of [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God), the [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul) and [prophetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prophecy) knowledge. Doctrines of the Arabic philosophers of the 9th–12th century who influenced medieval [Scholasticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism) in Europe. The Arabic tradition combines [Aristotelianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelianism) and [Neoplatonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplatonism) with other ideas introduced through [Islam](6037917.md). Influential thinkers include the non-Arabs [al-Farabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Farabi) and [Avicenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicenna). The Arabic philosophic literature was translated into [Hebrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hebrew_language) and [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin), this contributed to the development of modern European philosophy. The Arabic tradition was developed by [Moses Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moses_Maimonides) and [Ibn Khaldun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Khaldun).[^cite_note-496][^cite_note-497]

### Science

Main article: [Science in the medieval Islamic world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_in_the_medieval_Islamic_world)

See also: [Islamic world contributions to Medieval Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world_contributions_to_Medieval_Europe)



[Hevelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hevelius)'s *[Selenographia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selenographia)*, showing [Alhazen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alhazen)  \[*[sic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sic)*] representing reason, and [Galileo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei) representing the senses.

Arabic science underwent considerable development during the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages) (8th to 13th centuries CE), a [source of knowledge that later spread throughout Medieval Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_world_contributions_to_Medieval_Europe) and greatly influenced both medical practice and education. The language of recorded science was [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic). Scientific treatises were composed by thinkers originating from across the [Muslim world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world). These accomplishments occurred after [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) united the Arab tribes and the spread of Islam beyond the Arabian peninsula.[^cite_note-498]

Within a century after Muhammed's death (632 CE), an empire ruled by Arabs was established. It encompassed a large part of the planet, stretching from southern Europe to [North Africa](21714.md) to [Central Asia](6742.md) and on to India. In 711 CE, Arab Muslims invaded southern Spain; [al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus) was a center of Arabic scientific accomplishment. Soon after, Sicily too joined the greater Islamic world. Another center emerged in [Baghdad](4492.md) from the Abbasids, who ruled part of the [Islamic world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_world) during a historic period later characterized as the "[Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age)" (\~750 to 1258 CE).[^cite_note-499]

This era can be identified as the years between 692 and 945,[^cite_note-World_Civilization_Vol_1_1974,_pg._2342-500] and ended when the caliphate was marginalized by local Muslim rulers in Baghdad – its traditional seat of power. From 945 onward until the [sacking of Baghdad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sack_of_Baghdad) by the [Mongols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongol_invasions) in 1258, the Caliph continued on as a figurehead, with power devolving more to local subordinates.[^cite_note-World_Civilization_Vol_1_1974,_pg._2332-501] The pious scholars of Islam, men and women collectively known as the [ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama), were the most influential element of society in the fields of [Sharia](28840.md) law, speculative thought and theology.[^cite_note-502] Arabic scientific achievement is not as yet fully understood, but is very large.[^cite_note-RobinsonCambridge2-503] These achievements encompass a wide range of subject areas, especially [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics_in_medieval_Islam), [astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy_in_medieval_Islam), and [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine_in_medieval_Islam).[^cite_note-RobinsonCambridge2-503] Other subjects of scientific inquiry included [physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics_in_medieval_Islam), [alchemy and chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alchemy_and_chemistry_in_medieval_Islam), [cosmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmology_in_medieval_Islam), [ophthalmology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Islamic_ophthalmology), [geography and cartography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_and_cartography_in_medieval_Islam), [sociology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sociology_in_medieval_Islam), and [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology_in_medieval_Islam).[^cite_note-504]



Illustration of scholars dating from the Abbasid period by [Yahya ibn Mahmud al-Wasiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yahya_ibn_Mahmud_al-Wasiti) from the *[Maqamat of Al-Hariri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maqamat_of_Al-Hariri_\(manuscript\))* manuscript.

[Al-Battani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Battani) was an astronomer, astrologer and mathematician of the [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age). His work is considered instrumental in the development of science and astronomy. One of Al-Battani's best-known achievements in astronomy was the determination of the solar year as being 365 days, 5 hours, 46 minutes and 24 seconds which is only 2 minutes and 22 seconds off.[^cite_note-MacTutor2-505] In [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_mathematics), al-Battānī produced a number of [trigonometrical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometry) relationships.[^cite_note-MacTutor2-505] [Al-Zahrawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Zahrawi), regarded by many as the greatest surgeon of the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages).[^cite_note-506] His surgical treatise "[De chirurgia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Tasrif)" is the first illustrated surgical guide ever written. It remained the primary source for surgical procedures and instruments in Europe for the next 500 years.[^cite_note-507] The book helped lay the foundation to establish [surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surgery) as a scientific discipline independent from [medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicine), earning al-Zahrawi his name as one of the founders of this field.[^cite_note-508]

Other notable Arabic contributions include among other things: the pioneering of [organic chemistry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_chemistry) by [Jābir ibn Hayyān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C4%81bir_ibn_Hayy%C4%81n),[^cite_note-509] establishing the science of [cryptology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptology) and [cryptanalysis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryptanalysis) by [al-Kindi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Kindi),[^cite_note-510][^cite_note-511][^cite_note-512] the development of [analytic geometry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Analytic_geometry) by [Ibn al-Haytham](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Haytham),[^cite_note-513][^cite_note-514] who has been described as the "world's first true scientist",[^cite_note-515] the discovery of the [pulmonary circulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulmonary_circulation) by [Ibn al-Nafis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Nafis),[^cite_note-516][^cite_note-517] the discovery of the [itch mite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itch_mite) parasite by [Ibn Zuhr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Zuhr),[^cite_note-518] the first use of [irrational numbers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrational_numbers) as an algebraic objects by [Abū Kāmil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ab%C5%AB_K%C4%81mil),[^cite_note-519] the first use of the positional [decimal fractions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decimal_fractions) by [al-Uqlidisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abu'l-Hasan_al-Uqlidisi),[^cite_note-520][^cite_note-521] the development of the [Arabic numerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_numerals) and an early [algebraic symbolism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_mathematical_notation) in the [Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maghreb),[^cite_note-522][^cite_note-523] the [Thabit number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thabit_number) and [Thābit theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amicable_numbers#Th%C4%81bit_ibn_Qurra_theorem) by [Thābit ibn Qurra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C4%81bit_ibn_Qurra),[^cite_note-Rashed2-524] the discovery of several new [trigonometric identities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_trigonometric_identities) by [Ibn Yunus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Yunus) and [al-Battani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Battani),[^cite_note-525][^cite_note-526] the [mathematical proof](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_proof) for [Ceva's theorem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceva's_theorem) by [Ibn Hűd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yusuf_al-Mu'taman_ibn_Hud),[^cite_note-527] the invention of the [equatorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equatorium) by [al-Zarqali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Zarqali),[^cite_note-528] the discovery of the [physical reaction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reaction_\(physics\)) by [Avempace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avempace),[^cite_note-529] the identification of more than 200 new [plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plants) by [Ibn al-Baitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_al-Baitar)[^cite_note-530] the [Arab Agricultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Agricultural_Revolution), and the [Tabula Rogeriana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabula_Rogeriana), which was the most accurate world map in pre-modern times by [al-Idrisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Idrisi).[^cite_note-bacharach2-531]





Diagram of a hydro-powered perpetual flute from The Book of Knowledge of Ingenious Mechanical Devices by [Ismail al-Jazari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ismail_al-Jazari), 1206. (left) The eye according to [Hunayn ibn Ishaq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunayn_ibn_Ishaq), c.1200 (right)

Several universities and educational institutions of the Arab world such as the [University of al-Quarawiyyin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_al-Qarawiyyin), [Al-Azhar University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Azhar_University), and [Al Zaytuna University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Ez-Zitouna) are considered to be the oldest in the world. Founded by [Fatima al Fihriya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatima_al-Fihriya) in 859 as a mosque, the University of Al Quaraouiyine in [Fez](39760328.md) is the oldest existing, continually operating and the first [degree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_degree) awarding educational institution in the world according to [UNESCO](21786641.md) and [Guinness World Records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinness_World_Records)[^cite_note-532][^cite_note-533] and is sometimes referred to as the oldest university.[^cite_note-534]

There are many scientific [Arabic loanwords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Influence_of_Arabic_on_other_languages) in Western European languages, including [English](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Category\:English%20terms%20derived%20from%20Arabic), mostly via Old French.[^cite_note-535] This includes [traditional star names](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Arabic_star_names) such as [Aldebaran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aldebaran), scientific terms like *[alchemy](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/alchemy)* (whence also *[chemistry](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/chemistry)*), *[algebra](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/algebra)*, *[algorithm](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/algorithm)*, *[alcohol](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/alcohol)*, *[alkali](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/alkali)*, *[cipher](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/cipher)*, *[zenith](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/zenith)*, etc.

Under [Ottoman rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_rule), cultural life and science in the Arab world declined. In the 20th and 21st centuries, Arabs who have won important science prizes include [Ahmed Zewail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmed_Zewail) and [Elias Corey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elias_Corey) ([Nobel Prize](21201.md)), [Michael DeBakey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_DeBakey) and [Alim Benabid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alim_Louis_Benabid) ([Lasker Award](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lasker_Award)), [Omar M. Yaghi](17344326.md) ([Wolf Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wolf_Prize)), [Huda Zoghbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huda_Zoghbi) ([Shaw Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaw_Prize)), [Zaha Hadid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaha_Hadid) ([Pritzker Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pritzker_Prize)), and [Michael Atiyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Atiyah) (both [Fields Medal](10859.md) and [Abel Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Prize)). [Rachid Yazami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rachid_Yazami) was one of the co-inventors of the [lithium-ion battery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lithium-ion_battery),[^cite_note-536] and [Tony Fadell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tony_Fadell) was important in the development of the [iPod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPod) and the [iPhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPhone).[^cite_note-537]

### Theatre

Main article: [Experimental theatre in the Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Experimental_theatre_in_the_Arab_world)



[Youssef Wahbi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youssef_Wahbi), (1898–1982) was a prominent Arab playwright, actor, and director who played a major role in shaping modern Arab theatre.

Arab theatre is a rich and diverse cultural form that encompasses a wide range of styles, genres, and historical influences. Its roots in the pre-Islamic era, when poetry, storytelling, and musical performances were the main forms of artistic expressionIt refers to theatrical performances that are created by Arab playwrights, actors, and directors. The roots of Arab theatre can be traced back to ancient [Arabic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_poetry) and storytelling, which often incorporated music and dance. In the [early Arabic period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_early_Islam), storytelling evolved into a more formalized art form that was performed in public gatherings and festivals.[^cite_note-538][^cite_note-539]

During the [Islamic Golden Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Golden_Age) in the 8th and 9th centuries, the city of [Baghdad](4492.md) emerged as a hub of intellectual and artistic activity, including theatre. The court of the Abbasid Caliphate was home to many influential playwrights and performers, who helped to develop and popularize theatre throughout the Islamic world. Arab theatre has a long tradition of incorporating comedy and satire into its performances, often using humor to address social and political issues.[^cite_note-540]

Arab theatre encompasses a wide range of dramatic genres, including tragedy, melodrama, and historical plays. Many Arab playwrights have used drama to address contemporary issues, the role of [women in Arab society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_Arab_world), and the challenges facing young people in the modern world. In recent decades, many Arab theatre artists have pushed the boundaries of the form, experimenting with new styles and techniques. This has led to the emergence of a vibrant contemporary theatre scene in many Arab countries, with innovative productions and performances that challenge traditional notions of Arab identity and culture.[^cite_note-541]

### Fashion

Main article: [Arab Fashion Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Fashion_Council)



Modern [cross-stitch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-stitch) cushions. From top left, clockwise: [Gaza](55387.md), [Ramallah](44132.md), Ramallah, [Nablus](233369.md), [Beit Jalla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beit_Jalla), [Bethlehem](4312.md).

Arab fashion and design have a rich history and cultural significance that spans centuries, each with its unique fashion and design traditions. One of the most notable aspects of Arab fashion is the use of luxurious [fabrics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Textile) and intricate [embroidery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embroidery). Traditional garments, such as the [Abaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abaya) and [Thobe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thawb), are often made from high-quality fabrics like [silk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silk), [satin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satin), [brocade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brocade), and are embellished with intricate embroidery and beading.[^cite_note-542] In recent years, Arab fashion has gained global recognition, with designers like [Elie Saab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elie_Saab), [Zuhair Murad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zuhair_Murad), and [Reem Acra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reem_Acra) showcasing their designs on international runways.[^cite_note-543]

These designers incorporate traditional Arab design elements into their collections, such as ornate patterns, luxurious fabrics, and intricate embellishments. In addition to fashion, Arab design is also characterized by its intricate geometric [patterns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pattern), [calligraphy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_calligraphy), and use of vibrant colors. Arabic art and architecture, with their intricate geometric patterns and motifs, have influenced Arab design for centuries.[^cite_note-544] Arab designers also incorporate traditional motifs, such as the paisley and the [arabesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabesque), into their work. Overall, Arab fashion elements are rooted in the rich cultural heritage of the Arab world and continue to inspire designers today.[^cite_note-545]

### Wedding and marriage

Main article: [Arab wedding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_wedding)



[Henna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henna) tattoo in [Morocco](19291.md)

[Arabi weddings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_wedding) have changed greatly over the years. Original traditional Arab weddings have involved elements such as elaborate attire and traditional music, dance and ceremonies, and are in some cases unique from one region to another, even within the same country. The practice of [marrying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage) of relatives is a common feature of [Arab culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_culture).[^cite_note-546]

In the Arab world today, between 40% and 50% of all marriages are [consanguineous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consanguineous) or between close family members, though these figures may vary among Arab nations.[^cite_note-547][^cite_note-pmid242942992-548] In [Egypt](8087628.md), around 40% of the population [marry a cousin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cousin_marriage). A 1992 survey in [Jordan](7515964.md) found that 32% were married to a first cousin; a further 17.3% were married to more distant relatives.[^cite_note-549] 67% of marriages in [Saudi Arabia](349303.md) are between close relatives as are 54% of all marriages in [Kuwait](7515890.md), whereas 18% of all [Lebanese](17771.md) were between [blood relatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_relatives).[^cite_note-Inbreeding2-550] Due to the actions of [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) and the [Rashidun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun), marriage between cousins is explicitly allowed in [Islam](6037917.md) and the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) itself does not discourage or forbid the practice.[^cite_note-551] Nevertheless, opinions vary on whether the [phenomenon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenomenon) should be seen as exclusively based on Islamic practices as a 1992 study among Arabs in Jordan did not show significant differences between [Christian Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Christians) or [Muslim Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Muslims) when comparing the occurrence of [consanguinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consanguinity).[^cite_note-Inbreeding2-550]

## Genetics

See also: [Genetic studies on Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_studies_on_Arabs) and [Genetic history of the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_the_Middle_East)

Arabs are genetically diverse, arising from admixture with indigenous peoples of pre-Islamic Middle East and North Africa, following the Islamic expansion.[^cite_note-\:2-552][^cite_note-553] Genetic ancestry components related to the Arabian Peninsula display an increasing frequency pattern from west to east over North Africa. A similar frequency pattern exist across [northeastern Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_Africa) with decreasing genetic affinities to groups of the Arabian Peninsula along the [Nile river valley](21244.md) across [Sudan](27421.md) and [South Sudan](32350676.md) the more they go south.[^cite_note-\:3-554] This [genetic cline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cline_\(biology\)) of admixture is dated to the time of [Arab expansion and immigration to the Maghreb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_migrations_to_the_Maghreb) and northeast Africa.[^cite_note-\:3-554] Genetic research has indicated that Palestinian Arabs and [Jews](25955086.md) share common genetic ancestry and are closely related.[^cite_note-555][^cite_note-556][^cite_note-Nebel2000-557][^cite_note-Behar2010-558][^cite_note-559][^cite_note-560][^cite_note-561][^cite_note-Nebel_2001-562] According to a 2016 study, indigenous Arabs from the Arabian Peninsula are direct descendants of the first Eurasian populations established by [Out of Africa migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Out_of_Africa_migration). They are also very distant from contemporary Eurasians although there is signal of European admixture.[^cite_note-563]

Ancient DNA analysis has confirmed the genetic relationship between [Natufians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natufians) and other ancient and modern Middle Easterners and the broader West Eurasian meta-population (i.e. [Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_history_of_Europe) and [South-Central Asians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganj_Dareh)). A 2021 study found that some modern Arab groups, such as [Saudi Arabians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabians) and [Yemenis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yemenis), derive most of their ancestry from local Natufian hunter-gatherers and have less [Neolithic Anatolian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatolian_hunter-gatherers) ancestry than Levantines. The presence of [Neolithic Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iranian_hunter-gatherers) ancestry among modern Arabs can be attributed to [migrations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-modern_human_migration) during the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age).[^cite_note-564] The Natufian population displays also ancestral ties to Paleolithic [Taforalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taforalt) samples, the makers of the Epipaleolithic [Iberomaurusian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberomaurusian) culture of the Maghreb.[^cite_note-565]

Thus, Herod was of Arab origin, although he was a practicing Jew." Perowne, Stewart Henry (25 June 2015). [^cite_note-209]: &#x20;["Arabs"](http://www.iranicaonline.org/articles/abgar-dynasty-of-edessa-2nd-century-bc-to-3rd-century-ad) at *[Encyclopædia Iranica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A6dia_Iranica)*&#x54;he fame of Edessa in history rests, however, mainly on its claim to have been the first kingdom to adopt Christianity as its official religion. According to the legend current for centuries throughout the civilized world, Abgar Ukkama wrote to Jesus, inviting him to visit him at Edessa to heal him from sickness. In return he received the blessing of Jesus and subsequently was converted by the evangelist Addai. There is, however, no factual evidence for Christianity at Edessa before the reign of Abgar the Great, 150 years later. Scholars are generally agreed that the legend has confused the two Abgars. It cannot be proved that Abgar the Great adopted Christianity; but his friend Bardaiṣan was a heterodox Christian, and there was a church at Edessa in 201. It is testimony to the personality of Abgar the Great that he is credited by tradition with a leading role in the evangelization of Edessa.

[^cite_note-560]: &#x20;Nebel (2000), quote: By the fifth century CE, the majority of non-Jews and Jews had become Christians by conversion ([Bachi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Bachi) 1974). The first millennium CE was marked by the immigration of Arab tribes, reaching its climax with the Moslem conquest from the Arabian Peninsula (633–640 CE). This was followed by a slow process of Islamization of the local population, both of Christians and Jews (Shaban 1971; Mc Graw Donner 1981). Additional minor demographic changes might have been caused by subsequent invasions of the Seljuks, Crusaders, Mongols, Mamelukes and Ottoman Turks. Recent gene-flow from various geographic origins is reflected, for example, in the heterogeneous spectrum of globin mutations among Israeli Arabs (Filon et al. 1994). Israeli and Palestinian Arabs share a similar linguistic and geographic background with Jews. (p. 631) According to historical records part, or perhaps the majority, of the Moslem Arabs in this country descended from local inhabitants, mainly Christians and Jews, who had converted after the Islamic conquest in the seventh century CE (Shaban 1971; Mc Graw Donner 1981). These local inhabitants, in turn, were descendants of the core population that had lived in the area for several centuries, some even since prehistorical times (Gil 1992). On the other hand, the ancestors of the great majority of present-day Jews lived outside this region for almost two millennia. Thus, our findings are in good agreement with historical evidence and suggest genetic continuity in both populations despite their long separation and the wide geographic dispersal of Jews.(p.637)
